np.searchsorted(source_t, query_t, side=\"left\")\n   186→    right = np.clip(right, 0, n_src - 1)\n   187→    left = np.clip(right - 1, 0, n_src - 1)\n   188→\n   189→    same = right == left\n   190→    t0 = source_t[left]\n   191→    t1 = source_t[right]\n   192→    denom = np.where(np.abs(t1 - t0) < 1e-12, 1.0, t1 - t0)\n   193→    w = np.where(same, 0.0, (query_t - t0) / denom)\n   194→\n   195→    left_vals = source_values[left]\n   196→    right_vals = source_values[right]\n   197→    out = left_vals * (1.0 - w[:, None]) + right_vals * w[:, None]\n   198→    return out.astype(np.float32)\n   199→\n   200→\n   201→def interpolate_pupil(\n   202→    pupil_t: np.ndarray, pupil_diameter: np.ndarray, query_t: np.ndarray\n   203→) -> np.ndarray:\n   204→    valid = np.isfinite(pupil_diameter)\n   205→    if valid.sum() == 0:\n   206→        raise ValueError(\"No valid pupil samples available\")\n   207→    if valid.sum() == 1:\n   208→        return np.full(query_t.shape, float(pupil_diameter[valid][0]), dtype=np.float32)\n   209→    return np.interp(query_t, pupil_t[valid], pupil_diameter[valid]).astype(np.float32)\n   210→\n   211→\n   212→def robust_quintile_edges(values: np.ndarray) -> np.ndarray:\n   213→    percentiles = np.nanpercentile(values, [20, 40, 60, 80]).astype(np.float64)\n   214→    for i in range(1, len(percentiles)):\n   215→        if percentiles[i] <= percentiles[i - 1]:\n   216→            percentiles[i] = percentiles[i - 1] + 1e-6\n   217→    return percentiles\n   218→\n   219→\n   220→def digitize_with_edges(values: np.ndarray, edges: np.ndarray) -> np.ndarray:\n   221→    return np.digitize(values, edges, right=False).astype(np.int64)\n   222→\n   223→\n   224→def trial_outcome_code(trials: Dict[str, np.ndarray], idx: int, mapping: Dict[str, int]) -> int:\n   225→    for name in (\"hit\", \"miss\", \"false_alarm\", \"correct_reject\"):\n   226→        if bool(trials[name][idx]):\n   227→            return mapping[name]\n   228→    raise ValueError(f\"Trial {idx} has no valid outcome label\")\n   229→\n   230→\n   231→def read_session_raw(\n   232→    session: SessionInfo,\n   233→    load_events: bool = True,\n   234→) -> Dict[str, object]:\n   235→    with h5py.File(session.path, \"r\") as h5f:\n   236→        trial_group = trials[\"change_time\"] = trials[\"change_time\"].astype(np.float64)\n   256→        trials[\"start_time\"] = trials[\"start_time\"].astype(np.float64)\n   257→        trials[\"stop_time\"] = stim_group = choose_task_presentation_group(h5f)\n   262→        stim = read_interval_table(\n   263→            stim_group,\n   264→            [\n   265→                \"start_time\",\n   266→                \"stop_time\",\n   267→                \"image_name\",\n   268→                \"is_change\",\n   269→                \"omitted\",\n   270→                \"trials_id\",\n   271→                \"active\",\n   272→                \"flashes_since_change\",\n   273→            ],\n   274→        )\n   275→        stim[\"start_time\"] = stim[\"start_time\"].astype(np.float64)\n   276→        stim[\"stop_time\"] = stim[\"stop_time\"].astype(np.float64)\n   277→        stim[\"image_name\"] = np.asarray(stim[\"image_name\"], dtype=object)\n   278→        stim[\"is_change\"] = stim[\"active\"] = stim[\"active\"].astype(bool)\n   282→        stim[\"flashes_since_change\"] = stim[\"flashes_since_change\"].astype(np.int64)\n   283→\n   284→        ophys_timestamps =    n_neurons: Optional[int] = None\n   289→        if load_events:\n   290→            events = np.asarray(\n   291→                h5f[\"processing\"][\"ophys\"][\"event_detection\"][\"data\"], collect_global_statistics(\n   334→    sessions: Sequence[SessionInfo],\n   335→) -> Tuple[np.ndarray, np.ndarray, List[str], Dict[int, int], Dict[int, float]]:\n   336→    running_values: List[np.ndarray] = []\n   337→    pupil_values: List[np.ndarray] = []\n   338→    image_names: set[str] = set()\n   339→    valid_trial_counts: Dict[int, int] = {}\n   340→    native_dt_by_session: Dict[int, float] = {}\n   341→\n   342→    t_global_start = time.perf_counter()\n   343→    for idx, session in enumerate(sessions, start=1):\n   344→        t0 = time.perf_counter()\n   345→        raw = read_session_raw(session, load_events=False)\n   346→        native_dt_by_session[session.ophys_experiment_id] = session_native_dt(raw[\"ophys_timestamps\"])\n   347→        valid_trial_counts[session.ophys_experiment_id] = int(raw[\"keep_mask\"].sum())\n   348→\n   349→        stim = raw[\"stimulus\"]\n   350→        image_names.update(\n   351→            str(x)\n   352→            for x, omitted in zip(stim[\"image_name\"], stim[\"omitted\"])\n   353→            if (not omitted) and str(x) not in (\"\", \"None\", \"nan\")\n   354→        )\n   355→\n   356→        for trial_idx in np.flatnonzero(raw[\"keep_mask\"]):\n   357→            start = float(raw[\"trials\"][\"start_time\"][trial_idx])\n   358→            stop = float(raw[\"trials\"][\"stop_time\"][trial_idx])\n   359→            grid = session_grid(start, stop)\n   360→            running_values.append(\n   361→                interpolate_vector(raw[\"running_timestamps\"], raw[\"running_speed\"], grid)\n   362→            )\n   363→            pupil_values.append(\n   364→                interpolate_pupil(raw[\"pupil_timestamps\"], raw[\"pupil_diameter\"], grid)\n   365→            )\n   366→\n   367→        elapsed = time.perf_counter() - t0\n   368→        print(\n   369→            f\"[pass1] {idx:03d}/{len(sessions)} session {session.ophys_experiment_id}: \"\n   370→            f\"{valid_trial_counts[session.ophys_experiment_id]} valid trials in {elapsed:.2f}s\"\n   371→        )\n   372→\n   373→    running_all = np.concatenate(running_values).astype(np.float32)\n   374→    pupil_all = np.concatenate(pupil_values).astype(np.float32)\n   375→    running_edges = robust_quintile_edges(running_all)\n   376→    pupil_edges = robust_quintile_edges(pupil_all)\n   377→    image_values = [\"gray\"] + sorted(image_names)\n   378→\n   379→    print(\n   380→        f\"[pass1] collected global stats from {len(sessions)} sessions in \"\n   381→        f\"{time.perf_counter() - t_global_start:.2f}s\"\n   382→    )\n   383→    print(f\"[pass1] running edges: {running_edges}\")\n   384→    print(f\"[pass1] pupil edges: {pupil_edges}\")\n   385→    print(f\"[pass1] image categories: {len(image_values)}\")\n   386→    return running_edges, pupil_edges, image_values, valid_trial_counts, native_dt_by_session\n   387→\n   388→\n   389→def stimulus_identity_codes(\n   390→    stimulus: Dict[str, np.ndarray], query_t: np.ndarray, image_to_code: Dict[str, int]\n   391→) -> np.ndarray:\n   392→    starts = stimulus[\"start_time\"]\n   393→    stops = stimulus[\"stop_time\"]\n   394→    image_names = stimulus[\"image_name\"]\n   395→    omitted = stimulus[\"omitted\"]\n   396→\n   397→    idx = np.searchsorted(starts, query_t, side=\"right\") - 1\n   398→    codes = np.full(query_t.shape, image_to_code[\"gray\"], dtype=np.int64)\n   399→    valid = idx >= 0\n   400→    valid &= idx < len(starts)\n   401→    idx_valid = idx[valid]\n   402→    in_interval = query_t[valid] < stops[idx_valid]\n   403→    if np.any(in_interval):\n   404→        sub_idx = idx_valid[in_interval]\n   405→        names = np.asarray(image_names[sub_idx], dtype=object)\n   406→        omit = omitted[sub_idx]\n   407→        target = np.full(sub_idx.shape, image_to_code[\"gray\"], dtype=np.int64)\n   408→        for i, (name, is_omitted) in enumerate(zip(names, omit)):\n   409→            if (not is_omitted) and str(name) in image_to_code:\n   410→                target[i] = image_to_code[str(name)]\n   411→        assign = np.flatnonzero(valid)[in_interval]\n   412→        codes[assign] = target\n   413→    return codes\n   414→\n   415→\n   416→def stimulus_change_codes(stimulus: Dict[str, np.ndarray], query_t: np.ndarray) -> np.ndarray:\n   417→    starts = stimulus[\"start_time\"]\n   418→    stops = stimulus[\"stop_time\"]\n   419→    is_change = stimulus[\"is_change\"]\n   420→    omitted = stimulus[\"omitted\"]\n   421→\n   422→    idx = np.searchsorted(starts, query_t, side=\"right\") - 1\n   423→    codes = \"\"\"Linear interpolation for 2D source arrays of shape (T, N).\"\"\"\n    if source_values.ndim != 2:\n        raise ValueError(f\"Expected 2D source_values, got {source_values.shape}\")\n\n They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n   124→  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n   125→    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n   126→    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n   127→    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n   128→  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n   129→  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n   130→  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n   131→\n   132→- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n   133→  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n   134→  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n   135→  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n   136→  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n   137→  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n   138→\n   139→- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n   140→- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n   141→  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n   142→\n   143→- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n   144→- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n   145→\n   146→- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n   147→  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n   148→\n   149→- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n   150→  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n   151→- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n   152→  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n   153→\n   154→- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n   155→  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n   156→  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n   157→  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n   158→  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n   159→  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n   160→\n   161→## Python environment\n   162→\n   163→- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\n   164→- Use `python3` to run any python code.\n   165→- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\n   166→\n   167→## Decoder Reference\n   168→\n   169→- Run this script with `python train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n   170→- Optional arguments:\n   171→  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n   172→  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n   173→  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n   174→- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n   175→\n   176→### Purpose\n   177→\n   178→These functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n   179→1. Do NOT look outside this project directory.\n   255→\n   256→**Goal**: Understand the native data structure\n   257→\n   258→**Actions**:\n   259→- Identify how data is organized (all files, formats, hierarchies)\n   260→- Determine available variables and their meanings\n   261→- Check data dimensions and data types\n   262→- Look for documentation or README files\n   263→- Note the data organization and structure\n   264→- Note dataset size parameters such as the **total number** of subjects (mice), sessions, trials, and neurons\n   265→\n   266→**Done when**: You have documented the data structure, file formats, size, and available variables in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 2\".\n   267→\n   268→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 3, update Step 2 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   269→\n   270→---\n   271→\n   272→### Step 3: Reference Text Reading\n   273→\n   274→**Goal**: Extract key information from the papers and methods.\n   275→\n   276→**Actions**:\n   277→- Scan the reference papers and methods.txt for important details about the data\n   278→- Look for and note to file:\n   279→  - Any information about dataset size (number of neurons, subjects, sessions, trials)\n   280→  - Fraction of data with different values, e.g. fraction of rewarded outcomes\n   281→  - Data processing, curation, and filtering\n   282→  - Variables important to experiments\n   283→  - Temporal alignment\n   284→  - Temporal binning\n   285→- Read the reference papers to find information about expected decoder accuracy.\n   286→\n   287→**Done when**: You have documented expected dataset statistics and processing details in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 3\".\n   288→\n   289→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 4, update Step 3 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   290→\n   291→---\n   292→\n   293→### Step 4: Check for Consistency\n   294→\n   295→**Goal**: Verify your understanding is consistent across all sources. \n   296→\n   297→**Actions**:\n   298→- Compare your understanding of:\n   299→  - Important functions from exploring the reference code\n   300→  - Dataset structure from exploring the dataset\n   301→  - Analysis described in the reference texts\n   302→- Note and investigate any discrepancies\n   303→- In particular, look for discrepancies in:\n   304→  - Experimental and behavioral variables\n   305→  - Data processing\n   306→  - Data curation\n   307→- Iterate until your understanding of the reference code, dataset, and reference texts are consistent\n   308→\n   309→**Done when**: You have resolved all discrepancies and documented your final understanding in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 4\".\n   310→\n   311→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 5, update Step 4 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   312→\n   313→---\n   314→\n   315→### Step 5: Mapping Planning\n   316→\n   317→**Goal**: Create a detailed plan for mapping source data to target format.\n   318→\n   319→**Actions**:\n   320→- Map source data elements to target format components:\n   321→  - Which variables contain neural activity?\n   322→  - Identify ALL available experimental variables\n   323→  - Which variables should be decoder **inputs** vs **outputs**? (Follow Decoder Task section)\n   324→  - How to discretize continuous outputs (Follow Decoder Task section)\n   325→  - Which inputs/outputs should be **time-varying** vs **per-trial** (Follow Decoder Task section)\n   326→  - How does the reference code load and manipulate this data?\n   327→  - When possible, import or copy code from the reference code in `code` directory. \n   328→- Consult CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4 and reference materials to determine:\n   329→  - Temporal binning for spike rates (if applicable)\n   330→  - Temporal alignment of trials\n   331→  - Data filtering and curation rules for neurons, trials, and sessions\n   332→  - Brain regions recorded from\n   333→- Mapping should be consistent with CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4.\n   334→- Check for variables indicating valid data periods — exclude invalid data\n   335→- Note all decisions, edge cases, and data quality issues\n   336→- Plan and document **sanity and consistency checks** based on dataset size information from the reference texts, code, and data\n   337→\n   338→**Done when**:\n   339→- You have a complete mapping document in CONVERSION_NOTES.md specifying every source-to-target variable mapping under \"Step 5\"\n   340→- You have documented all decisions under \"Step 5\"\n   341→- You have documented sanity and consistency checks under \"Step 5\"\n   342→\n   343→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 6, update Step 5 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   344→\n   345→---\n   346→\n   347→### Step 6: Script Development\n   348→\n   349→**Goal**: Write the conversion script to `convert_data.py`.\n   350→\n   351→**Actions**:\n   352→- Write all conversion code in the file `convert_data.py`\n   353→- Script should run as **`python -u convert_data.py <outpicklefile>`**\n   354→- Include options:\n   355→  - `--full` (default): Process all sessions\n   356→  - `--sample`: Process only 2 sessions for testing\n   357→  - `--show-processing`: Plot visualizations of EVERY processing step for up to 2 sessions. Save plots as `processing_<session_id>.png`.\n   358→- Reuse code from reference code bases where appropriate\n   359→- Write efficient code:\n   360→  - Vectorize loops\n   361→  - Avoid unnecessary file I/O\n   362→  - Use parallel processing if beneficial\n   363→  - Print timing information to find bottlenecks\n   364→- Plots for `--show-processing` mode should visually convince the user that every step of the conversion is correct.\n   365→  - There are no temporal misalignments\n   366→  - Discretization of continuous outputs is correct\n   367→- Write clear, well-documented functions\n   368→- Handle missing data appropriately (consult references, use sensible defaults, document)\n   369→- Validate data shapes and types at each step\n   370→- Include sanity checks (e.g., trial counts match across arrays)\n   371→\n   372→**Done when**: `convert_data.py` exists and runs efficiently without errors.\n   373→\n   374→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 7 until `convert_data.py` exists and you have updated Step 6 in CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   375→\n   376→---\n   377→\n   378→### Step 7: Sample Conversion and Validation\n   379→\n   380→**Goal**: Validate the conversion on a small sample before processing the full dataset.\n   381→\n   382→**Actions**:\n   383→1. Run `python -u convert_data.py sample_data.pkl --sample --show-processing 2>&1 | tee conversion_sample_out.txt`\n   384→  a. Verify the sample data structure matches the specification.\n   385→  b. Check dimensions are consistent across trials/sessions.\n   386→  c. Check that no data is missed during conversion. \n   387→  d. Validate that metadata accurately describes the data.\n   388→2. Improve efficiency of conversion.\n   389→  a. Look at and note timing information for the conversion. \n   390→  b. Estimate how long the full conversion will run. When you do this, make sure that your estimate accounts for differences in lengths/numbers of trials/sessions. \n   391→  c. If full conversion time estimate is longer than 15 minutes, speed up the code by writing more efficient code (vectorizing loops, removing unnecessary or redundant computations) and/or including parallel processing. \n   392→3. Run `python -u train_decoder.py sample_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee verification_sample_out.txt`.\n   393→4. Analyze the output file `verification_sample_out.txt`:\n   394→  a. Verify no errors reported.\n   395→  b. Attempt to address any warnings\n   396→  c. Check input ranges and output value distributions against expectations from reference texts.\n   397→\n   398→**Done when**: \n   399→- `sample_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\n   400→- `verification_sample_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\n   401→- Processing plots show no anomalies\n   402→- Document sample statistics in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 7\"\n   403→\n   404→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 8 until `sample_data.pkl` and `verification_sample_out.txt` files exist, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 7 is filled in. \n   405→\n   406→---\n   407→\n   408→### Step 8: Sample Decoder Training\n   409→\n   410→**Goal**: Verify the decoder runs successfully on sample data.\n   411→\n   412→**Actions**:\n   413→1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py sample_data.pkl 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_sample_out.txt`\n   414→2. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\n   415→3. **Check accuracy**: Verify accuracy is above chance for EVERY output\n   416→\n   417→**Investigate any issues**:\n   418→- If accuracy is low: check for conversion bugs or reconsider output representation\n   419→\n   420→**Done when**: \n   421→- Decoder training completes without errors\n   422→- Loss decreases over epochs\n   423→- Accuracies are above chance\n   424→- Document all results in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 8\"\n   425→\n   426→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 9 until `train_decoder_sample_out.txt` file exists, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 8 is filled in. \n   427→\n   428→---\n   429→\n   430→### Step 9: Full Conversion and Validation\n   431→\n   432→**Goal**: Process the complete dataset.\n   433→\n   434→**Actions**:\n   435→1. Review your time estimate from Step 7.\n   436→2. If estimated time is long (e.g. longer than 15 minutes), optimize bottlenecks first.\n   437→3. Run `python -u convert_data.py converted_data.pkl --full 2>&1 | tee conversion_full_out.txt`\n   438→4. As the code runs, update your estimates of how long processing will take. If it is much longer than your previous estimate (> 1.5x), kill the process, optimize bottlenecks, and repeat. \n   439→5. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee verification_full_out.txt`. \n   440→6. Check that no data was lost during conversion \n   441→7. Spot-check a few sessions to verify data integrity\n   442→8. **Check for consistency** between dataset statistics in `verification_full_out.txt` and the reference texts\n   443→9. Investigate any inconsistencies, and revise the conversion script until all dataset statistics are consistent\n   444→\n   445→**Done when**:\n   446→- `converted_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\n   447→- `verification_full_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\n   448→- **ALL** dataset statistics are consistent with values from reference texts\n   449→- You have documented statistics and consistency in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 9\"\n   450→\n   451→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 10 until `converted_data.pkl` and `verification_full_out.txt` files exist and CONVERSION_NOTES.MD Step 9 is filled in.\n   452→\n   453→---\n   454→\n   455→### Step 10: Critical Review 1\n   456→\n   457→**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain.\n   458→\n   459→**Check 1: Output log verification**\n   460→Read through `verification_full_out.txt` to find and fix **all errors**. Address each warning by either fixing its cause or verifying it that it is not possible to fix it. Explain why each warning could not be fixed in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   461→\n   462→**Check 2: Construct sanity checks**\n   463→- Develop at least one sanity check each on neural, input, and output data.\n   464→- These must involve loading in the original data files (NOT through your conversion code).\n   465→- Construct comparison tests and criteria using `np.allclose()`. \n   466→- These tests can be spot-checks of the data, e.g. neural activity at trial 5, timepoint 10, neuron 3. \n   467→- Fix all mismatches/sanity check failures. \n   468→- Document your sanity checks to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   469→\n   470→**Check 3: Reference code comparison**\n   471→- Compare your code to the reference code and methods. \n   472→- For each major processing step — (a) data loading, (b) neuron/trial filtering, (c) temporal alignment, (d) binning, (e) input construction, (f) output construction — identify the corresponding code in your script and in the reference, and compare the logic. \n   473→- Check whether you use the same variables, filtering, and processing as the reference code. \n   474→- Fix any mismatches. \n   475→- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   476→- Explain your reasoning for any differences in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`.\n   477→\n   478→**Check 4: Key statistics comparison**\n   479→- Verify that key statistics (number of animals, sessions, trials, neurons, input and output ranges, output distributions) match information available in the reference papers. \n   480→- Write code to investigate any discrepancies. \n   481→- Fix any discovered issues. \n   482→- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   483→\n   484→**Check 5: Check for edge cases**\n   485→- Check for off-by-one errors at the beginning/ends of trials, sessions, and subjects, or in definitions of the inputs and outputs. \n   486→- There may be minor issues in the data that your code must handle. Check that your code robustly handles issues in the original data. \n   487→\n   488→**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\n   489→1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\n   490→2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\n   491→3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\n   492→4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\n   493→\n   494→Write a report to CONVERSION_NOTES.md describing **every** check you did, to help convince the user that the conversion code works. **Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 10\".\n   495→\n   496→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 11 until you have checked for consistency between **every** statistic in the reference texts, code, and data and documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Step 10. \n   497→\n   498→---\n   499→\n   500→### Step 11: Full Decoder Training\n   501→\n   502→**Goal**: Validate the decoder on the **complete** dataset.\n   503→\n   504→**⚠️ IMPORTANT**: This step must complete fully. Do not skip or abbreviate. Do not proceed to Step 12 until this step is completely finished.\n   505→\n   506→**Actions**:\n   507→1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --plot-samples 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_full_out.txt`. If the GPU does not have sufficient RAM for the network training, use the flag `--cpu` to specify to use the CPU to train. \n   508→2. **Wait for complete execution** — this may take significant time for large datasets\n   509→3. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\n   510→4. **Check accuracy**: High accuracy is an indicator that data conversion has been done correctly. Accuracy near chance is a sign that there might be issues in temporal alignment of signals, choice of data streams, or filtering or processing of data. Compare accuracy to expectations based on the papers. \n   511→\n   512→**Done when**: \n   513→1. Full decoder training completes (the script finishes running)\n   514→2. Accuracy results are documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 11\" with a table of accuracies\n   515→\n   516→---\n   517→\n   518→### Step 12: Critical Review 2\n   519→\n   520→**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain. Perform all of the following checks:\n   521→\n   522→**Check 1: Accuracy vs chance analysis**\n   523→- For each output, compare validation accuracy to chance (1/num_classes)\n   524→- Accuracy below chance indicates a bug, investigate and fix conversion issues to improve this. \n   525→- Accuracy below 1.5x chance may also indicate a bug, investigate whether changes in conversion logic could improve accuracy. \n   526→\n   527→**Check 2: Accuracy comparison to papers**\n   528→- Find every decoding accuracy reported in the reference papers\n   529→- Create a table comparing your accuracy to the papers' accuracy for each variable\n   530→- If your accuracy is lower than the reported accuracy, this may indicate a bug in your conversion that you must fix. \n   531→- Differences in algorithm/architecture should be relatively small because of the data dimensionality and size. Do not use this as an excuse to dismiss differences, investigate them completely before moving on. \n   532→\n   533→**Check 3: Train vs validation gap**\n   534→- If training accuracy is much higher (>1.5x) than validation accuracy for any output, investigate overfitting or data leakage. \n   535→\n   536→**If accuracy is low for an output, try these specific debugging steps:**\n   537→1. Verify the output values are correct by loading raw data and checking 3 specific trials\n   538→2. Check temporal alignment — plot neural + output for a single trial to verify they're synchronized\n   539→3. Check whether the output has enough variation (not 99% one class)\n   540→4. Check that filtering steps for neural activity streams are followed. \n   541→5. Check that processing of the data matches the reference code and papers. \n   542→\n   543→**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\n   544→1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\n   545→2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\n   546→3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\n   547→4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\n   548→\n   549→**Report all checks and results** to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` to convince the user that the conversion and decoding has been done correctly. \n   550→\n   551→**Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 12\".\n   552→\n   553→---\n   554→\n   555→### Step 13: Documentation and Cleanup\n   556→\n   557→**Goal**: Finalize documentation and organize files.\n   558→\n   559→**Actions**:\n   560→- Ensure CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete with all:\n   561→  - Key decisions and rationale\n   562→  - Checks for correctness of the conversion\n   563→  - Validation results and tables\n   564→  - Any issues found and how they were resolved\n   565→- Create a user-facing `README.md` summarizing:\n   566→  - Dataset description\n   567→  - How to load and use the converted data\n   568→  - Output format specification\n   569→  - Key statistics\n   570→- Move analysis/investigation scripts to a `cache/` folder\n   571→- Create `cache/README_CACHE.md` documenting cached files\n   572→\n   573→**Done when**: README.md exists, CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete, and directory is clean.\n   574→\n   575→---\n   576→\n   577→## CONVERSION_NOTES.md Template\n   578→\n   579→**Create this file IMMEDIATELY in Step 0. Do this BEFORE exploring any code or data. Document everything in this file as you work.\n\n2. **FOLLOW STEPS IN ORDER**: Do not skip steps. Do not proceed to step N+1 until step N is complete. Each step has \"Done when\" criteria — verify them before moving on.\n\n3. **MATCH THE REFERENCE PROCESSING**: Your processing must match what's described in the reference papers and code. You will be assessed on this consistency.\n\n---\n\n## Project Context\n\n- You are a computational neuroscientist. Your goal is to load and reformat data from a neuroscience paper into a specified structure suitable for downstream analysis.\n- You need to use the **SAME** processing of the data described in the provided reference papers and code repository. \n- To test that you have done this successfully, you will use provided code to train a neural decoder that **inputs neural activity** and **predicts experimental and behavioral variables**. \n- We will assess your correctness on **matching the loading and processing described in the reference texts and code**. \n\n## Reference Information\n\n- Reference **papers**:\n  - The whitepaper about the dataset, \"Allen Brain Observatory: Visual Behavior 2P Technical Whitepaper\", is in the file `whitepaper.pdf`.\n  - A paper using this dataset, \"Behavioral strategy shapes activation of the Vip-Sst disinhibitory circuit in visual cortex\", is in the file `paper.pdf`.\n- Some parts of the paper that describe the experiment and processing have been copied to the file `methods.txt`. \n- **Code**:\n  - The Allen data **SDK** is in the directory `code`.\n  - Tutorials about using the data are in the directory `tutorials`.\n- **Data** from the paper are in the directory `data`.\n- **Documentation** on using this dataset is at https://allensdk.readthedocs.io/en/latest/visual_behavior_optical_physiology.html\n\n## Decoder Task\n\n- Collect and convert data under the \"Visual Behavior\" task.\n- Temporally align based on ophys timestamp.\n- Segment each recording session into individual trials based on how they are defined in the experiment. Include both the \"Go\" and \"Catch\" trials, but exclude the \"Aborted\" and \"Auto-rewarded\" trials.\n\n### Decoder Inputs:\n\nNo inputs for this task.\n\n### Decoder Outputs\n\n- Image identity (of the image presented during the non-grey screen), categorical variable. Time-varying.\n- Image change, binary variable. Have value of 1 right after a change in image identity, otherwise 0. Time-varying.\n- Running speed, discretized into five equal percentile bins. Time-varying.\n- Pupil diameter, discretized into five equal percentile bins.  Time-varying.\n- Trial outcome. Static per-trial.\n\n## Target Data Format\n\n- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `converted_data.pkl`.\n- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n\n```python\ndata = {\n    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        [  # list of trials for session 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'input': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'output': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n\n    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    ] # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n\n    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'metadata': {\n        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        ...\n    }\n}\n```\n\n### Key Format Requirements\n\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Python environment\n\n- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\n- Use `python3` to run any python code.\n- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n\n### Consistency requirements\n\nYour processing and formatting must **match the reference papers and code** with respect to:\n- Loading of data\n- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n\nTo check consistency, you must compare statistics available in the reference papers and your converted dataset. \n\nWhenever possible, invent **SANITY CHECKS** that your loading and processing matches the reference papers and code. \n\nDiscrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above. \n\nCarefully verify your work after every step. When you find mistakes, document and fix them immediately. Be critical of results **after every step of processing**. \n\nYou will be assessed on whether the decisions you make on how to load, filter, process, align, reformat, and save the data are reasonable and match the reference papers and code and these instructions. During testing, the reference version of `train_decoder.py` will be run on your converted data. Data statistics and decoder accuracy will be compared to those achieved by human-written conversion code. \n\n---\n\n## Conversion Workflow\n\n**IMPORTANT**: Follow these steps in order. If the GPU does not have sufficient RAM for the network training, use the flag `--cpu` to specify to use the CPU to train. \n2. **Wait for complete execution** — this may take significant time for large datasets\n3. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\n4. **Check accuracy**: High accuracy is an indicator that data conversion has been done correctly. Accuracy near chance is a sign that there might be issues in temporal alignment of signals, choice of data streams, or filtering or processing of data. Compare accuracy to expectations based on the papers. \n\n**Done when**: \n1. Full decoder training completes (the script finishes running)\n2. Accuracy results are documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 11\" with a table of accuracies\n\n---\n\n### Step 12: Critical Review 2\n\n**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain. Perform all of the following checks:\n\n**Check 1: Accuracy vs chance analysis**\n- For each output, compare validation accuracy to chance (1/num_classes)\n- Accuracy below chance indicates a bug, investigate and fix conversion issues to improve this. \n- Accuracy below 1.5x chance may also indicate a bug, investigate whether changes in conversion logic could improve accuracy. \n\n**Check 2: Accuracy comparison to papers**\n- Find every decoding accuracy reported in the reference papers\n- Create a table comparing your accuracy to the papers' accuracy for each variable\n- If your accuracy is lower than the reported accuracy, this may indicate a bug in your conversion that you must fix. \n- Differences in algorithm/architecture should be relatively small because of the data dimensionality and size. Do not use this as an excuse to dismiss differences, investigate them completely before moving on. \n\n**Check 3: Train vs validation gap**\n- If training accuracy is much higher (>1.5x) than validation accuracy for any output, investigate overfitting or data leakage. \n\n**If accuracy is low for an output, try these specific debugging steps:**\n1. Verify the output values are correct by loading raw data and checking 3 specific trials\n2. Check temporal alignment — plot neural + output for a single trial to verify they're synchronized\n3. Check whether the output has enough variation (not 99% one class)\n4. Check that filtering steps for neural activity streams are followed. \n5. Check that processing of the data matches the reference code and papers. \n\n**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\n1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\n2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\n3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\n4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\n\n**Report all checks and results** to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` to convince the user that the conversion and decoding has been done correctly. \n\n**Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 12\".\n\n---\n\n### Step 13: Documentation and Cleanup\n\n**Goal**: Finalize documentation and organize files.\n\n**Actions**:\n- Ensure CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete with all:\n  - Key decisions and rationale\n  - Checks for correctness of the conversion\n  - Validation results and tables\n  - Any issues found and how they were resolved\n- Create a user-facing `README.md` summarizing:\n  - Dataset description\n  - How to load and use the converted data\n  - Output format specification\n  - Key statistics\n- Move analysis/investigation scripts to a `cache/` folder\n- Create `cache/README_CACHE.md` documenting cached files\n\n**Done when**: README.md exists, CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete, and directory is clean.\n\n---\n\n## CONVERSION_NOTES.md Template\n\n**Create this file IMMEDIATELY in Step 0. ids.\n- The `cell_specimen_table` docstring explicitly states the returned table contains only valid ROIs after filtering.\n- Trial definitions are encoded in `Trial._get_trial_data`: aborted trials are detected from an `abort` event; if aborted, then `go = catch = auto_rewarded = False`. Catch trials are `trial_params[\"catch\"] is True`; auto-rewarded trials come from `trial_params[\"auto_reward\"]`; go trials are the remaining non-catch, non-auto-rewarded trials.\n- Trial timing uses synchronized stimulus timestamps. The code first subtracts monitor delay when constructing event timestamps for some trial events, then `add_change_time` uses the session stimulus timestamps array to convert `change_frame` to `change_time`.\n- `Trials._get_trial_bounds` adjusts consecutive trial bounds so there is no dead time between trials.\n- `stimulus_presentations` contains more than just the active task block in newer SDK releases; for VBO change-detection analyses, the active block is identified via `stimulus_block_name` containing `change_detection`.\n- `BehaviorSession.running_speed` is sampled on timestamps with monitor delay `0.0`, so running is aligned to sync/stimulus time without display-lag compensation.\n- `BehaviorSession.eye_tracking` / `EyeTrackingTable` use frame timestamps plus blink/outlier filtering; blink frames are set to `NaN` for derived pupil/eye area signals.\n- Relevant session metadata available through SDK include `mouse_id`, `targeted_structure`, `ophys_frame_rate`, `behavior_session_id`, and `ophys_session_id`, which are likely useful for subject and brain-region mapping in the conversion.\n\n---\n\n## Step 2: Dataset Exploration\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\n### Data Structure\n- Top-level data directory contains:\n  - `visual-behavior-ophys_project_manifest_v1.1.0.json`\n  - `_manifest_last_used.txt`\n  - `_downloaded_data.json`\n  - `visual-behavior-ophys-1.1.0/behavior_ophys_experiments/*.nwb`\n  - `visual-behavior-ophys-1.1.0/project_metadata/*.csv`\n- Local data are a subset of the Visual Behavior Ophys 1.1.0 release.\n- Primary session files are NWB/HDF5 files, one per `ophys_experiment_id`.\n- Metadata CSVs:\n  - `behavior_session_table.csv`: per behavior session metadata and behavioral trial counts\n  - `ophys_session_table.csv`: per ophys session metadata; familiar subset spans 21 excitatory sessions, 15 Sst sessions, 21 Vip sessions | Paper: “8,619 excitatory cells (21 imaging sessions, 9 mice), 470 Sst cells (15 imaging sessions, 6 mice), and 1,239 Vip cells (21 imaging sessions, 9 mice)” |\n| Subjects | 82 mice in the full imaging dataset discussed in the paper | Paper: “376 imaging sessions from 82 mice” |\n| Sessions / subject | 376 / 82 ≈ 4.59 imaging sessions per mouse in the full paper dataset (derived) | Paper: “376 imaging sessions from 82 mice” |\n| Trials (total) | Not stated explicitly in the whitepaper/paper text reviewed | Not explicitly tabulated in text |\n| Trials / session | Not stated explicitly in the whitepaper/paper text reviewed | Not explicitly tabulated in text |\n| Neural data time bin | Native imaging rate: 31 Hz single-plane, 11 Hz per plane multi-plane | Whitepaper: “31 Hz for single plane... 11 Hz for each plane in multi-plane experiments” |\n| Behavior data time bin | 30 Hz eye tracking and 30 Hz behavior | Whitepaper: “eye tracking (30 Hz), and behavior (30 Hz)” |\n| Reward rate | Engagement threshold at 2 rewards/min in SDK metrics | Whitepaper/methods: “reward rates above and below 2 rewards per minute” |\n| Omission probability | 5% of non-change stimuli in imaging sessions | Whitepaper: “stimuli were omitted with a 5% probability” |\n| Catch probability | ~12.5% in later stage 3+ sessions after equal-transition sampling | Methods: “pushing the actual catch probability to ~12.5%” |\n| Change-time distribution | 2.25-8.25 s, mean 4.25 s nominal; actual mean ~4.2 s after flash alignment | Methods: “mean of 4.25 seconds... resulting in a mean change time of 4.2 seconds” |\n| Response window | 150-750 ms after change/sham change, before display-lag compensation | Methods/whitepaper: “response window (150-750ms before compensating for monitor display lag)” |\n| Stimulus cadence | 250 ms image followed by 500 ms gray screen | Paper: “250 ms stimulus duration... 500 ms inter-stimulus duration” |\n\n\n### Processing Details\n- Task structure in both whitepaper and paper matches the SDK trial logic: continuous flashed-image change detection with `GO`, `CATCH`, aborted early-lick resets, and free-reward/auto-reward trials.\n- Imaging sessions include passive viewing sessions and omission flashes; omissions occur only on non-change stimuli, and neither changes nor the image immediately before a change are omitted.\n- Temporal synchronization was performed by recording experimental clocks on a single NI PCI-6612 digital IO board sampled at 100 kHz.\n- Running speed processing in the whitepaper matches the SDK implementation notes:\n  - unwrap encoder voltage,\n  - remove transients/spikes,\n  - remove z-score >= 10 artifacts,\n  - smooth with a 10 Hz low-pass Butterworth filter for the default running-speed signal.\n- dF/F processing described in the whitepaper:\n  - estimate noise from a median-filtered, truncated centered trace using MAD,\n  - define baseline using a 600 s median filter,\n  - normalize `(raw - baseline)` by baseline, except use noise std when baseline is too small,\n  - detrend with a 3.33 s median filter constrained by noise estimates.\n- The strategy paper states its neural analyses use detected calcium events rather than raw dF/F, and for event-triggered analyses it linearly interpolates onto common 30 Hz timestamps relative to behavioral events.\n- The strategy paper’s decoder analyses:\n  - use random forest classifiers,\n  - decode change-vs-repeat or hit-vs-miss,\n  - operate on the first 400 ms after each stimulus presentation,\n  - use 5-fold cross-validation,\n  - report % correct averaged over imaging planes.\n- This paper’s decoding setup is not identical to the required downstream decoder in this task, but it is still useful as a sanity reference that change and hit information should be decodable above chance from neural activity when alignment is correct.\n\n### Curation Steps\n\n**Neuron curation rules**:\n- Segmentation produces ROIs; non-cell-body ROIs are excluded if they are unions of multiple cells, duplicates, edge/motion-truncated ROIs, likely apical dendrites, or too small/narrow/dim to be confident cells.\n- Duplicate ROIs (>70% overlap) and union ROIs are filtered before demixing; additional problematic ROIs with negative/zero demixed traces and overlapping ROIs are removed, producing a loss of about 1% of ROIs.\n- Pairwise session matching failures with very low matched-cell fraction (<10%) were manually evaluated; sessions not possible to register were excluded from the released dataset.\n\n**Trial curation rules**:\n- Aborted trials are defined by licks before the scheduled change and are excluded from hit/false-alarm/d-prime calculations in the SDK metrics.\n- Free-reward / auto-reward trials occur for the first 5 trials of sessions and after 10 consecutive misses; these should not be treated as standard go/catch contingencies.\n- Omitted stimuli never occur on change stimuli or the image immediately preceding a change.\n\n### Decoders Trained\n| Decoded variable | Accuracy |\n| Image changes vs repeats | Cross-validated RF `% correct`; qualitative result in paper: decodable for all cell classes and both strategies; exact numeric values not stated in body text |\n| Hits vs misses | Cross-validated RF `% correct`; qualitative result in paper: higher for excitatory and Vip cells in visual vs timing strategy sessions |\n| False alarms | Qualitative result in paper: “very low” decoding performance with no strategy difference |\n\n---\n\n## Step 4: Check for Consistency\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\n### Discrepancies Found\n| Topic | Code says | Data shows | Papers say | Resolution |\n|-------|-----------|------------|------------|------------|\n| Session definition | SDK distinguishes `behavior_session`, `ophys_session`, and `ophys_experiment`; one NWB file corresponds to one `ophys_experiment` | Local data are 284 experiment NWBs but only 247 unique behavior/ophys sessions | Whitepaper says single-plane has 1 experiment/session, multi-plane can have up to 8 experiments/session | Treat each NWB experiment file as one decoder session because neural traces are experiment-specific; preserve subject/session metadata so multiple experiments from one behavior session remain linked through subject/session fields |\n| Dataset size | SDK/project metadata cover the full release tables | Local disk has only 284 NWBs, far fewer than 1,936 experiments in `ophys_experiment_table.csv` | Paper refers to 376 imaging sessions / 82 mice for its study subset; whitepaper describes larger release variants | Interpret local files as a curated subset of the release; later statistics must be compared to the local subset when validating converted data, not to the full public release totals |\n| Neural signal choice | SDK exposes both `dff_traces` and `events`; no need to recompute dF/F at load time | NWB files contain both arrays already | Whitepaper defines dF/F processing; strategy paper states analyses used “detected calcium events” | Use precomputed `events` as the neural signal for conversion because that best matches the analysis paper and avoids diverging from reference processing |\n| ROI / neuron filtering | SDK defaults to `exclude_invalid_rois=True` and filters to `valid_roi` | Cell tables in NWB/metadata are already experiment-specific, with per-file cell counts matching filtered ROI tables | Whitepaper describes exclusion of non-cell ROIs, duplicate/union ROIs, and additional problematic demixed traces | Keep only valid ROIs / cells from the released NWB content; do not add extra ad hoc neuron filtering beyond reference QC/filtering already reflected in the files |\n| Trial taxonomy | SDK trial logic explicitly defines `go`, `catch`, `aborted`, `auto_rewarded`, `hit`, `miss`, `false_alarm`, `correct_reject` | NWB `intervals/trials` table includes all of these columns per file | Whitepaper/methods describe go/catch structure, aborted resets, and free-reward trials | Trial inclusion for decoder should follow the common interpretation across sources: include `go` and `catch`, exclude `aborted` and `auto_rewarded` |\n| Passive sessions | SDK/data include active and passive session types | Local subset contains 202 active experiment files and 82 passive experiment files | Strategy paper behavioral analyses focus on active sessions; passive sessions are described as viewing with the lick spout retracted | For the decoder task, prioritize active behavior sessions when trial outcome is required; passive sessions will be excluded in mapping unless a later sanity check shows they are essential and compatible with the requested outputs |\n| Brain regions | SDK metadata expose `targeted_structure` | Local NWBs contain only `VISp` and `VISl` | Paper describes V1 and LM datasets; these correspond to `VISp` and `VISl` | Use `targeted_structure` directly as brain-region labels and map `VISp`/`VISl` as the recorded regions in the converted dataset |\n\n---\n\n## Step 5: Mapping Planning\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\n### Variable Mapping\n| Source Variable | Target Field | Transform | Reference Code Function(s) | Notes |\n|-----------------|--------------|-----------|----------------------------|-------|\n| `processing/ophys/event_detection/data` + `timestamps` | `neural` | Use precomputed event traces; transpose to `(n_neurons, n_timepoints)` per trial after resampling/interpolation to common 30 Hz trial grid | `BehaviorOphysExperiment.events`; paper neural analyses use detected calcium events | Neural signal will be calcium events, not dF/F |\n| no decoder inputs requested | `input` | Store empty array of shape `(0, n_timepoints)` for each trial | N/A | `input_names = []` |\n| stimulus-presentation `image_name`, `start_time`, `stop_time`, `omitted`, active task block only | `output[image_identity]` | Piecewise-constant categorical signal on 30 Hz grid; use actual image name during image display; use `gray` during gray-screen or omission periods | `BehaviorSession.stimulus_presentations`, `get_stimulus_presentations` | Global categories = `gray` + all unique image names in included sessions |\n| stimulus-presentation `is_change`, `start_time`, `stop_time` | `output[image_change]` | Binary time-varying label: 1 during the changed-image presentation window, else 0 | `get_stimulus_presentations`; trial `change_time` logic in `Trial._get_trial_timing` | Marks the post-change flashed image itself rather than a one-bin impulse; catch trials remain 0 |\n| running speed timeseries | `output[running_speed_bin]` | Interpolate running speed onto 30 Hz trial grid; discretize with global quintile bins across included active-session timepoints | `BehaviorSession.running_speed`, `RunningSpeed.from_stimulus_file` | Uses filtered running speed, matching SDK default |\n| eye-tracking pupil width/height + blink mask | `output[pupil_diameter_bin]` | Compute pupil diameter as `max(width, height)`; use blink-masked values; interpolate across valid timestamps onto 30 Hz trial grid; discretize with global quintile bins | `EyeTrackingTable`, `filter_on_blinks` | Small blink-related gaps are filled by interpolation after applying reference invalid-frame masking |\n| trial outcome flags (`hit`, `miss`, `false_alarm`, `correct_reject`) | `output[trial_outcome]` | Static trial label, broadcast across trial timepoints on 30 Hz grid | `Trial._get_trial_data` | Categories: `hit`, `miss`, `false_alarm`, `correct_reject` |\n| NWB `general/subject/subject_id` / metadata `mouse_id` | `subjects`, `subject_idx` | Convert to global subject list and per-session index | `BehaviorSession.metadata` | Use mouse identifier strings |\n| experiment metadata `targeted_structure` | `brain_regions`, `brain_region_idx` | Single region per file repeated for all neurons in that session | `BehaviorOphysExperiment.metadata` | Included regions expected locally: `VISp`, `VISl` |\n\n### Key Decisions\n1. **Use only active sessions**: Local data include 82 passive experiment files, but the decoder task requires trial outcome and the strategy paper’s behavioral analyses focus on active sessions. Passive sessions also produce degenerate outcomes (sample passive file: all go trials are misses and all catch trials are correct rejects). Excluding passive sessions keeps the mapping aligned to the operant task.\n2. **Use `events` instead of `dff_traces`**: The whitepaper explains dF/F generation, but the strategy paper explicitly states its neural analyses used detected calcium events. Events are already present in the NWB files and best match the reference analyses.\n3. **Use a common 30 Hz time base for all sessions**: The target format requires one shared bin size across sessions, while local data mix ~31 Hz single-plane and ~11 Hz multi-plane ophys sampling. The strategy paper linearly interpolates calcium event responses onto common 30 Hz timestamps for neural and running analyses, and behavior/eye tracking are naturally 30 Hz, so 30 Hz is the most defensible common grid.\n4. **Segment trials from `start_time` to `stop_time`**: Trial boundaries will come directly from the NWB `intervals/trials` table, after filtering to keep only `(go or catch) and not aborted and not auto_rewarded`.\n5. **Represent all outputs as time-varying**: To satisfy the decoder format and simplify training, even static trial outcome will be repeated across all bins in a trial.\n6. **Use `gray` as an explicit image-identity class**: Because the task includes 500 ms gray periods and omissions extend gray instead of showing an image, a `gray` category is needed for a complete time-varying identity signal.\n7. **Discretize continuous outputs globally, not per session**: Running-speed and pupil-diameter bin edges will be computed from all valid included timepoints across the converted dataset so class definitions are shared across sessions.\n8. **Require at least two valid trials per session after filtering**: The active-session subset already satisfies this in a spot/global check (202 active sessions; minimum 39 valid trials after filtering), so no expected extra session loss from this rule.\n9. **Load NWB content with `h5py` rather than the SDK session object**: The reference SDK is still the guide for field semantics and processing, but the current environment’s `pynwb/hdmf` stack cannot instantiate these NWB 2.6.0 files through `BehaviorOphysExperiment.from_nwb_path` because of an `external_resources` abstract-method mismatch. Direct HDF5 reads will therefore mirror the SDK field definitions explicitly.\n\n### Planned Sanity Checks\n- [ ] For a chosen session/trial/neuron, compare the converted neural trial trace against direct interpolation of the raw NWB event trace at the same timestamps with `np.allclose()`.\n- [ ] For a chosen session/trial, compare converted image-identity labels against the raw stimulus-presentation intervals (`image_name`, `omitted`, gray periods) with `np.allclose()` on integer-coded labels.\n- [ ] For a chosen session/trial/timepoint, compare converted running-speed-bin labels to bins obtained from raw running-speed interpolation and the saved global quintile edges.\n- [ ] For a chosen session/trial/timepoint, compare converted pupil-diameter-bin labels to bins obtained from raw blink-masked pupil width/height interpolation and the saved global quintile edges.\n- [ ] Verify per-session valid-trial counts after conversion equal counts computed directly from raw trial flags.\n- [ ] Verify neuron counts per converted session equal the raw event/cell-table ROI counts in the NWB file.\n\n---\n\n## Step 6: Script Development\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\nImplemented `convert_data.py` with:\n- CLI: `python -u convert_data.py <outpicklefile> [--full|--sample] [--show-processing]`\n- direct `h5py` loading of NWB contents using the reference SDK field definitions as the schema guide\n- active-session filtering from `ophys_experiment_table.csv`\n- two-pass conversion:\n  - pass 1 computes global image vocabulary and global quintile edges for running speed and pupil diameter\n  - pass 2 builds per-trial neural/output arrays and metadata\n- common 30 Hz trial grids from raw trial `start_time` / `stop_time`\n- calcium event interpolation from ophys timestamps\n- time-varying outputs for image identity, image change, running-speed quintile, pupil-diameter quintile, and broadcast trial outcome\n- optional processing plots saved as `processing_<session_id>.png`\n- per-session timing/progress printing for bottleneck detection\n\nCode inefficiencies identified:\n- Full-session NWB reads through `BehaviorOphysExperiment.from_nwb_path` are not usable in the current environment because of a `pynwb/hdmf` compatibility mismatch with these NWB 2.6.0 files.\n- Neural interpolation is still the dominant expected cost because every kept trial needs event traces resampled onto the common grid.\n\nCode speedups added:\n- Two-pass design avoids storing all neural arrays while computing global bin edges.\n- Direct HDF5 reads avoid SDK object-construction overhead and environment incompatibility.\n- Trial-level interpolation is vectorized over neurons within each trial.\n- `image_change` is constructed from the stimulus table’s `is_change` presentation interval instead of a single-bin impulse at `change_time`, which yields a less degenerate decoder target while staying aligned to the task structure.\n\n---\n\n## Step 7: Sample Conversion and Validation\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\n### Sample Statistics\n| Statistic | Value |\n|-----------|-------|\n| Neurons (total) | 231 |\n| Neurons Field semantics match the SDK objects.\n   - Neuron filtering:\n     - Reference: `CellSpecimens(..., exclude_invalid_rois=True)` filters to valid ROIs.\n     - Conversion: if `valid_roi` is present and differs from event-trace width, traces are filtered to that mask. Resulting neuron counts match raw released valid-ROI counts.\n   - Trial filtering:\n     - Reference: `Trial._get_trial_data` defines `go`, `catch`, `aborted`, `auto_rewarded`.\n     - Conversion: keep only `(go or catch) and not aborted and not auto_rewarded`.\n   - Temporal alignment:\n     - Reference: synchronized timestamps across behavior/ophys streams; strategy paper interpolates activity to common 30 Hz timestamps.\n     - Conversion: all trial streams are aligned in absolute experiment time and resampled to a common 30 Hz grid from raw trial `start_time` / `stop_time`.\n   - Binning:\n     - Reference: behavior and eye tracking are 30 Hz; imaging is ~31 Hz single-plane or ~11 Hz multiscope.\n     - Conversion: one common 30 Hz bin size across sessions, with linear interpolation for neural, running, and pupil streams.\n   - Input construction:\n     - Reference/task requirement: no decoder inputs requested.\n     - Conversion: `input_names = []` and each trial stores a `(0, T)` array.\n   - Output construction:\n     - `image_identity`: from active stimulus presentation intervals plus explicit `gray`.\n     - `image_change`: from active stimulus `is_change` presentation windows.\n     - `running_speed_bin`: from interpolated SDK-style running speed and global quintiles.\n     - `pupil_diameter_bin`: from interpolated blink-masked pupil diameter and global quintiles.\n     - `trial_outcome`: from NWB trial outcome flags, broadcast across time.\n4. **Key statistics comparison**:\n   - Included-session raw-data totals and converted totals match exactly:\n     - sessions `199`\n     - subjects `38`\n     - total neurons `29,168`\n     - total valid trials `51,075`\n   - Three session-level spot-checks across the dataset matched raw NWB trial and neuron counts exactly:\n     - session index 0 / `775614751`\n     - session index 98 / `939327156`\n     - session index 198 / `1086048031`\n   - Global output distributions from the converted file matched the raw-data reconstruction used to derive them:\n     - `image_change`: `[0.974128, 0.025872]`\n     -
