familiar subset spans 21 excitatory sessions, 15 Sst sessions, 21 Vip sessions | Paper: “8,619 excitatory cells (21 imaging sessions, 9 mice), 470 Sst cells (15 imaging sessions, 6 mice), and 1,239 Vip cells (21 imaging sessions, 9 mice)” |\n   118→| Subjects | 82 mice in the full imaging dataset discussed in the paper | Paper: “376 imaging sessions from 82 mice” |\n   119→| Sessions / subject | 376 / 82 ≈ 4.59 imaging sessions per mouse in the full paper dataset (derived) | Paper: “376 imaging sessions from 82 mice” |\n   120→| Trials (total) | Not stated explicitly in the whitepaper/paper text reviewed | Not explicitly tabulated in text |\n   121→| Trials / session | Not stated explicitly in the whitepaper/paper text reviewed | Not explicitly tabulated in text |\n   122→| Neural data time bin | Native imaging rate: 31 Hz single-plane, 11 Hz per plane multi-plane | Whitepaper: “31 Hz for single plane... 11 Hz for each plane in multi-plane experiments” |\n   123→| Behavior data time bin | 30 Hz eye tracking and 30 Hz behavior | Whitepaper: “eye tracking (30 Hz), and behavior (30 Hz)” |\n   124→| Reward rate | Engagement threshold at 2 rewards/min in SDK metrics | Whitepaper/methods: “reward rates above and below 2 rewards per minute” |\n   125→| Omission probability | 5% of non-change stimuli in imaging sessions | Whitepaper: “stimuli were omitted with a 5% probability” |\n   126→| Catch probability | ~12.5% in later stage 3+ sessions after equal-transition sampling | Methods: “pushing the actual catch probability to ~12.5%” |\n   127→| Change-time distribution | 2.25-8.25 s, mean 4.25 s nominal; actual mean ~4.2 s after flash alignment | Methods: “mean of 4.25 seconds... resulting in a mean change time of 4.2 seconds” |\n   128→| Response window | 150-750 ms after change/sham change, before display-lag compensation | Methods/whitepaper: “response window (150-750ms before compensating for monitor display lag)” |\n   129→| Stimulus cadence | 250 ms image followed by 500 ms gray screen | Paper: “250 ms stimulus duration... 500 ms inter-stimulus duration” |\n   130→\n   131→\n   132→### Processing Details\n   133→- Task structure in both whitepaper and paper matches the SDK trial logic: continuous flashed-image change detection with `GO`, `CATCH`, aborted early-lick resets, and free-reward/auto-reward trials.\n   134→- Imaging sessions include passive viewing sessions and omission flashes; omissions occur only on non-change stimuli, and neither changes nor the image immediately before a change are omitted.\n   135→- Temporal synchronization was performed by recording experimental clocks on a single NI PCI-6612 digital IO board sampled at 100 kHz.\n   136→- Running speed processing in the whitepaper matches the SDK implementation notes:\n   137→  - unwrap encoder voltage,\n   138→  - remove transients/spikes,\n   139→  - remove z-score >= 10 artifacts,\n   140→  - smooth with a 10 Hz low-pass Butterworth filter for the default running-speed signal.\n   141→- dF/F processing described in the whitepaper:\n   142→  - estimate noise from a median-filtered, truncated centered trace using MAD,\n   143→  - define baseline using a 600 s median filter,\n   144→  - normalize `(raw - baseline)` by baseline, except use noise std when baseline is too small,\n   145→  - detrend with a 3.33 s median filter constrained by noise estimates.\n   146→- The strategy paper states its neural analyses use detected calcium events rather than raw dF/F, and for event-triggered analyses it linearly interpolates onto common 30 Hz timestamps relative to behavioral events.\n   147→- The strategy paper’s decoder analyses:\n   148→  - use random forest classifiers,\n   149→  - decode change-vs-repeat or hit-vs-miss,\n   150→  - operate on the first 400 ms after each stimulus presentation,\n   151→  - use 5-fold cross-validation,\n   152→  - report % correct averaged over imaging planes.\n   153→- This paper’s decoding setup is not identical to the required downstream decoder in this task, but it is still useful as a sanity reference that change and hit information should be decodable above chance from neural activity when alignment is correct.\n   154→\n   155→### Curation Steps\n   156→\n   157→**Neuron curation rules**:\n   158→- Segmentation produces ROIs; non-cell-body ROIs are excluded if they are unions of multiple cells, duplicates, edge/motion-truncated ROIs, likely apical dendrites, or too small/narrow/dim to be confident cells.\n   159→- Duplicate ROIs (>70% overlap) and union ROIs are filtered before demixing; additional problematic ROIs with negative/zero demixed traces and overlapping ROIs are removed, producing a loss of about 1% of ROIs.\n   160→- Pairwise session matching failures with very low matched-cell fraction (<10%) were manually evaluated; sessions not possible to register were excluded from the released dataset.\n   161→\n   162→**Trial curation rules**:\n   163→- Aborted trials are defined by licks before the scheduled change and are excluded from hit/false-alarm/d-prime calculations in the SDK metrics.\n   164→- Free-reward / auto-reward trials occur for the first 5 trials of sessions and after 10 consecutive misses; these should not be treated as standard go/catch contingencies.\n   165→- Omitted stimuli never occur on change stimuli or the image immediately preceding a change.\n   166→\n   167→### Decoders Trained\n   168→| Decoded variable | Accuracy |\n   169→| Image changes vs repeats | Cross-validated RF `% correct`; qualitative result in paper: decodable for all cell classes and both strategies; exact numeric values not stated in body text |\n   170→| Hits vs misses | Cross-validated RF `% correct`; qualitative result in paper: higher for excitatory and Vip cells in visual vs timing strategy sessions |\n   171→| False alarms | Qualitative result in paper: “very low” decoding performance with no strategy difference |\n   172→\n   173→---\n   174→\n   175→## Step 4: Check for Consistency\n   176→**Status**: COMPLETE\n   177→\n   178→### Discrepancies Found\n   179→| Topic | Code says | Data shows | Papers say | Resolution |\n   180→|-------|-----------|------------|------------|------------|\n   181→| Session definition | SDK distinguishes `behavior_session`, `ophys_session`, and `ophys_experiment`; one NWB file corresponds to one `ophys_experiment` | Local data are 284 experiment NWBs but only 247 unique behavior/ophys sessions | Whitepaper says single-plane has 1 experiment/session, multi-plane can have up to 8 experiments/session | Treat each NWB experiment file as one decoder session because neural traces are experiment-specific; preserve subject/session metadata so multiple experiments from one behavior session remain linked through subject/session fields |\n   182→| Dataset size | SDK/project metadata cover the full release tables | Local disk has only 284 NWBs, far fewer than 1,936 experiments in `ophys_experiment_table.csv` | Paper refers to 376 imaging sessions / 82 mice for its study subset; whitepaper describes larger release variants | Interpret local files as a curated subset of the release; later statistics must be compared to the local subset when validating converted data, not to the full public release totals |\n   183→| Neural signal choice | SDK exposes both `dff_traces` and `events`; no need to recompute dF/F at load time | NWB files contain both arrays already | Whitepaper defines dF/F processing; strategy paper states analyses used “detected calcium events” | Use precomputed `events` as the neural signal for conversion because that best matches the analysis paper and avoids diverging from reference processing |\n   184→| ROI / neuron filtering | SDK defaults to `exclude_invalid_rois=True` and filters to `valid_roi` | Cell tables in NWB/metadata are already experiment-specific, with per-file cell counts matching filtered ROI tables | Whitepaper describes exclusion of non-cell ROIs, duplicate/union ROIs, and additional problematic demixed traces | Keep only valid ROIs / cells from the released NWB content; do not add extra ad hoc neuron filtering beyond reference QC/filtering already reflected in the files |\n   185→| Trial taxonomy | SDK trial logic explicitly defines `go`, `catch`, `aborted`, `auto_rewarded`, `hit`, `miss`, `false_alarm`, `correct_reject` | NWB `intervals/trials` table includes all of these columns per file | Whitepaper/methods describe go/catch structure, aborted resets, and free-reward trials | Trial inclusion for decoder should follow the common interpretation across sources: include `go` and `catch`, exclude `aborted` and `auto_rewarded` |\n   186→| Passive sessions | SDK/data include active and passive session types | Local subset contains 202 active experiment files and 82 passive experiment files | Strategy paper behavioral analyses focus on active sessions; passive sessions are described as viewing with the lick spout retracted | For the decoder task, prioritize active behavior sessions when trial outcome is required; passive sessions will be excluded in mapping unless a later sanity check shows they are essential and compatible with the requested outputs |\n   187→| Brain regions | SDK metadata expose `targeted_structure` | Local NWBs contain only `VISp` and `VISl` | Paper describes V1 and LM datasets; these correspond to `VISp` and `VISl` | Use `targeted_structure` directly as brain-region labels and map `VISp`/`VISl` as the recorded regions in the converted dataset |\n   188→\n   189→---\n   190→\n   191→## Step 5: Mapping Planning\n   192→**Status**: COMPLETE\n   193→\n   194→### Variable Mapping\n   195→| Source Variable | Target Field | Transform | Reference Code Function(s) | Notes |\n   196→|-----------------|--------------|-----------|----------------------------|-------|\n   197→| `processing/ophys/event_detection/data` + `timestamps` | `neural` | Use precomputed event traces; transpose to `(n_neurons, n_timepoints)` per trial after resampling/interpolation to common 30 Hz trial grid | `BehaviorOphysExperiment.events`; paper neural analyses use detected calcium events | Neural signal will be calcium events, not dF/F |\n   198→| no decoder inputs requested | `input` | Store empty array of shape `(0, n_timepoints)` for each trial | N/A | `input_names = []` |\n   199→| stimulus-presentation `image_name`, `start_time`, `stop_time`, `omitted`, active task block only | `output[image_identity]` | Piecewise-constant categorical signal on 30 Hz grid; use actual image name during image display; use `gray` during gray-screen or omission periods | `BehaviorSession.stimulus_presentations`, `get_stimulus_presentations` | Global categories = `gray` + all unique image names in included sessions |\n   200→| stimulus-presentation `is_change`, `start_time`, `stop_time` | `output[image_change]` | Binary time-varying label: 1 during the changed-image presentation window, else 0 | `get_stimulus_presentations`; trial `change_time` logic in `Trial._get_trial_timing` | Marks the post-change flashed image itself rather than a one-bin impulse; catch trials remain 0 |\n   201→| running speed timeseries | `output[running_speed_bin]` | Interpolate running speed onto 30 Hz trial grid; discretize with global quintile bins across included active-session timepoints | `BehaviorSession.running_speed`, `RunningSpeed.from_stimulus_file` | Uses filtered running speed, matching SDK default |\n   202→| eye-tracking pupil width/height + blink mask | `output[pupil_diameter_bin]` | Compute pupil diameter as `max(width, height)`; use blink-masked values; interpolate across valid timestamps onto 30 Hz trial grid; discretize with global quintile bins | `EyeTrackingTable`, `filter_on_blinks` | Small blink-related gaps are filled by interpolation after applying reference invalid-frame masking |\n   203→| trial outcome flags (`hit`, `miss`, `false_alarm`, `correct_reject`) | `output[trial_outcome]` | Static trial label, broadcast across trial timepoints on 30 Hz grid | `Trial._get_trial_data` | Categories: `hit`, `miss`, `false_alarm`, `correct_reject` |\n   204→| NWB `general/subject/subject_id` / metadata `mouse_id` | `subjects`, `subject_idx` | Convert to global subject list and per-session index | `BehaviorSession.metadata` | Use mouse identifier strings |\n   205→| experiment metadata `targeted_structure` | `brain_regions`, `brain_region_idx` | Single region per file repeated for all neurons in that session | `BehaviorOphysExperiment.metadata` | Included regions expected locally: `VISp`, `VISl` |\n   206→\n   207→### Key Decisions\n   208→1. **Use only active sessions**: Local data include 82 passive experiment files, but the decoder task requires trial outcome and the strategy paper’s behavioral analyses focus on active sessions. Passive sessions also produce degenerate outcomes (sample passive file: all go trials are misses and all catch trials are correct rejects). Excluding passive sessions keeps the mapping aligned to the operant task.\n   209→2. **Use `events` instead of `dff_traces`**: The whitepaper explains dF/F generation, but the strategy paper explicitly states its neural analyses used detected calcium events. Events are already present in the NWB files and best match the reference analyses.\n   210→3. **Use a common 30 Hz time base for all sessions**: The target format requires one shared bin size across sessions, while local data mix ~31 Hz single-plane and ~11 Hz multi-plane ophys sampling. The strategy paper linearly interpolates calcium event responses onto common 30 Hz timestamps for neural and running analyses, and behavior/eye tracking are naturally 30 Hz, so 30 Hz is the most defensible common grid.\n   211→4. **Segment trials from `start_time` to `stop_time`**: Trial boundaries will come directly from the NWB `intervals/trials` table, after filtering to keep only `(go or catch) and not aborted and not auto_rewarded`.\n   212→5. **Represent all outputs as time-varying**: To satisfy the decoder format and simplify training, even static trial outcome will be repeated across all bins in a trial.\n   213→6. **Use `gray` as an explicit image-identity class**: Because the task includes 500 ms gray periods and omissions extend gray instead of showing an image, a `gray` category is needed for a complete time-varying identity signal.\n   214→7. **Discretize continuous outputs globally, not per session**: Running-speed and pupil-diameter bin edges will be computed from all valid included timepoints across the converted dataset so class definitions are shared across sessions.\n   215→8. **Require at least two valid trials per session after filtering**: The active-session subset already satisfies this in a spot/global check (202 active sessions; minimum 39 valid trials after filtering), so no expected extra session loss from this rule.\n   216→9. **Load NWB content with `h5py` rather than the SDK session object**: The reference SDK is still the guide for field semantics and processing, but the current environment’s `pynwb/hdmf` stack cannot instantiate these NWB 2.6.0 files through `BehaviorOphysExperiment.from_nwb_path` because of an `external_resources` abstract-method mismatch. Direct HDF5 reads will therefore mirror the SDK field definitions explicitly.\n   217→\n   218→### Planned Sanity Checks\n   219→- [ ] For a chosen session/trial/neuron, compare the converted neural trial trace against direct interpolation of the raw NWB event trace at the same timestamps with `np.allclose()`.\n   220→- [ ] For a chosen session/trial, compare converted image-identity labels against the raw stimulus-presentation intervals (`image_name`, `omitted`, gray periods) with `np.allclose()` on integer-coded labels.\n   221→- [ ] For a chosen session/trial/timepoint, compare converted running-speed-bin labels to bins obtained from raw running-speed interpolation and the saved global quintile edges.\n   222→- [ ] For a chosen session/trial/timepoint, compare converted pupil-diameter-bin labels to bins obtained from raw blink-masked pupil width/height interpolation and the saved global quintile edges.\n   223→- [ ] Verify per-session valid-trial counts after conversion equal counts computed directly from raw trial flags.\n   224→- [ ] Verify neuron counts per converted session equal the raw event/cell-table ROI counts in the NWB file.\n   225→\n test_verify_data_format(metrics, submitted_data_sample, submitted_data_full):\n   164→    \"\"\"Helper function to test data format and record metrics.\"\"\"\n   165→    valid, errors, warnings = verify_data_format(submitted_data_sample)\n   166→    metrics[\"sample_data_format_valid\"] = valid\n   167→    metrics[\"sample_data_format_errors\"] = errors\n   168→ Errors: {errors}\"\n   179→    if warnings:\n   180→        print(f\"Full data format warnings: {warnings}\")\n   181→\n   182→def match_variables_by_hungarian(submitted_ranges, reference_ranges,\n   183→                                submitted_fractions=None, reference_fractions=None,\n   184→                                exact_range=False, weight_semantic=.95):\n   185→    \"\"\"Find 1-1 matching between variables using the Hungarian algorithm.\n   186→\n   187→    If a submitted name exactly matches a reference name, they are forced to match\n   188→    with cost 0 (and all other pairings involving those names get cost inf).\n   189→\n   190→    For unmatched names, cost depends on mode:\n   191→    - exact_range=False: scaled range distance (max of normalized endpoint differences)\n   192→    - exact_range=True: requires exact range match (inf if not), then uses mean absolute\n   193→      difference of sorted fractions as cost. Requires submitted_fractions and\n   194→      reference_fractions.\n   195→\n   196→    Args:\n   197→        submitted_ranges: dict of {name: [min, max]}\n   198→        reference_ranges: dict of {name: [min, max]}\n   199→        submitted_fractions: dict of {name: {val: frac, ...}} (required if exact_range=True)\n   200→        reference_fractions: dict of {name: {val: frac, ...}} (required if exact_range=True)\n   201→        exact_range: if True, ranges must match exactly and fractions are used for cost\n   202→\n   203→    Returns:\n   204→        matches: list of (ref_name, sub_name, cost) tuples\n   205→        match_costs: list of costs for each chosen assignment\n   206→    \"\"\"\n   207→    ref_names = list(reference_ranges.keys())\n   208→    sub_names = list(submitted_ranges.keys())\n   209→    n = len(ref_names)\n   210→    m = len(sub_names)\n   211→\n   212→    if n != m:\n   213→        # Cannot match — return all-inf matches\n   214→        matches = [(rn, None, float('inf')) for rn in ref_names]\n   215→        match_costs = [float('inf')] * n\n   216→        return matches, match_costs\n   217→\n   218→    # Find exact name matches\n   219→    matched_ref = set()\n   220→    matched_sub = set()\n   221→    for i, rn in enumerate(ref_names):\n   222→        for j, sn in enumerate(sub_names):\n   223→            if rn == sn:\n   224→                matched_ref.add(i)\n   225→                matched_sub.add(j)\n   226→\n   227→    if not hasattr(match_variables_by_hungarian, 'SemanticMatchModel'):\n   228→        match_variables_by_hungarian.SemanticMatchModel = SentenceTransformer('all-MiniLM-L6-v2')  # fast, good quality, 80MB\n   229→    model = match_variables_by_hungarian.SemanticMatchModel\n   230→    # replace _ and - with spaces\n   231→    ref_sentences = [rn.replace('_', ' ').replace('-', ' ') for rn in ref_names]\n   232→    sub_sentences = [sn.replace('_', ' ').replace('-', ' ') for sn in sub_names]\n   233→    ref_emb = model.encode(ref_sentences)  # (N, 384)\n   234→    sub_emb = model.encode(sub_sentences)  # (M, 384)\n   235→\n   236→    cost = np.full((n, n), np.inf) if exact_range else np.zeros((n, n))\n   237→    for i, rn in enumerate(ref_names):\n   238→        for j, sn in enumerate(sub_names):\n   239→            if rn == sn:\n   240→                cost[i, j] = 0.0\n   241→            elif i in matched_ref or j in matched_sub:\n   242→                cost[i, j] = np.inf\n   243→            else:\n   244→                cost_semantic = 1 - util.cos_sim(ref_emb[i], sub_emb[j]).item()\n   245→                if exact_range:\n   246→                    ref_lo, ref_hi = reference_ranges[rn]\n   247→                    sub_lo, sub_hi = submitted_ranges[sn]\n   248→                    if ref_lo != sub_lo or ref_hi != sub_hi:\n   249→                        continue\n   250→                    ref_fracs = sorted(reference_fractions[rn].values())\n   251→                    sub_fracs = sorted(submitted_fractions[sn].values())\n   252→                    if len(ref_fracs) != len(sub_fracs):\n   253→                        continue\n   254→                    cost_fracs = np.sum(np.abs(np.array(ref_fracs) - np.array(sub_fracs)))\n   255→                    \n   256→                    cost[i, j] = cost_fracs*(1-weight_semantic) + cost_semantic*weight_semantic\n   257→                else:\n   258→                    ref_lo, ref_hi = reference_ranges[rn]\n   259→                    sub_lo, sub_hi = submitted_ranges[sn]\n   260→                    scale = max(abs(ref_hi - ref_lo), 1e-6)\n   261→                    cost_range = np.maximum(abs(ref_lo - sub_lo)/scale, abs(ref_hi - sub_hi)/scale)\n   262→                    cost[i, j] = cost_range*(1-weight_semantic) + cost_semantic*weight_semantic\n   263→\n   264→    row_ind, col_ind = linear_sum_assignment(cost)\n   265→    matches = []\n   266→    match_costs = []\n   267→    for i, j in zip(row_ind, col_ind):\n   268→        matches.append((ref_names[i], sub_names[j], float(cost[i, j])))\n   269→        match_costs.append(float(cost[i, j]))\n   270→    return matches, match_costs\n   271→\n   272→\n   273→\n   274→# def old_match_variables_by_hungarian(submitted_ranges, reference_ranges,\n   275→#                                 submitted_fractions=None, reference_fractions=None,\n   276→#                                 exact_range=False):\n   277→#     \"\"\"Find 1-1 matching between variables using the Hungarian algorithm.\n   278→#\n   279→#     If a submitted name exactly matches a reference name, they are forced to match\n   280→#     with cost 0 (and all other pairings involving those names get cost inf).\n   281→#\n   282→#     For unmatched names, cost depends on mode:\n   283→#     - exist but are empty: {empty}\")\n\ndef test_verify_data_format(metrics, submitted_data_sample, submitted_data_full):\n    \"\"\"Helper function to test data format and record metrics.\"\"\"\n    valid, errors, warnings = verify_data_format(submitted_data_sample)\n    metrics[\"sample_data_format_valid\"] = valid\n    metrics[\"sample_data_format_errors\"] = errors\n    metrics[\"sample_data_format_warnings\"] = warnings\n\n    assert valid, f\"Sample data format is invalid. Errors: {errors}\"\n    if warnings:\n        print(f\"Sample data format warnings: {warnings}\")\n        \n    valid, errors, warnings = verify_data_format(submitted_data_full)\n    metrics[\"full_data_format_valid\"] = valid\n    metrics[\"full_data_format_errors\"] = errors\n    metrics[\"full_data_format_warnings\"] = warnings\n    assert valid, f\"Full data format is invalid. Errors: {errors}\"\n    if warnings:\n        print(f\"Full data format warnings: {warnings}\")\n\ndef match_variables_by_hungarian(submitted_ranges, reference_ranges,\n                                submitted_fractions=None, reference_fractions=None,\n                                exact_range=False, weight_semantic=.95):\n    \"\"\"Find 1-1 matching between variables using the Hungarian algorithm.\n\n    If a submitted name exactly matches a reference name, they are forced to match\n    with cost 0 (and all other pairings involving those names get cost inf).\n\n    For unmatched names, cost depends on mode:\n    - exact_range=False: scaled range distance (max of normalized endpoint differences)\n    - exact_range=True: requires exact range match (inf if not), then uses mean absolute\n      difference of sorted fractions as cost. Requires submitted_fractions and\n      reference_fractions.\n\n    Args:\n        submitted_ranges: dict of {name: [min, max]}\n        reference_ranges: dict of {name: [min, max]}\n        submitted_fractions: dict of {name: {val: frac, ...}} (required if exact_range=True)\n        reference_fractions: dict of {name: {val: frac, ...}} (required if exact_range=True)\n        exact_range: if True, ranges must match exactly and fractions are used for cost\n\n    Returns:\n        matches: list of (ref_name, sub_name, cost) tuples\n        match_costs: list of costs for each chosen assignment\n    \"\"\"\n    ref_names = list(reference_ranges.keys())\n    sub_names = list(submitted_ranges.keys())\n    n = len(ref_names)\n    m = len(sub_names)\n\n    if n != m:\n        # Cannot match — return all-inf matches\n        matches = [(rn, None, float('inf')) for rn in ref_names]\n        match_costs = [float('inf')] * n\n        return matches, match_costs\n\n    # Find exact name matches\n    matched_ref = set()\n    matched_sub = set()\n    for i, rn in enumerate(ref_names):\n        for j, sn in enumerate(sub_names):\n            if rn == sn:\n                matched_ref.add(i)\n                matched_sub.add(j)\n\n    if not hasattr(match_variables_by_hungarian, 'SemanticMatchModel'):\n        match_variables_by_hungarian.SemanticMatchModel = SentenceTransformer('all-MiniLM-L6-v2')  # fast, good quality, 80MB\n    model = match_variables_by_hungarian.SemanticMatchModel\n    # replace _ and - with spaces\n    ref_sentences = [rn.replace('_', ' ').replace('-', ' ') for rn in ref_names]\n    sub_sentences = [sn.replace('_', ' ').replace('-', ' ') for sn in sub_names]\n    ref_emb = model.encode(ref_sentences)  # (N, 384)\n    sub_emb = model.encode(sub_sentences)  # (M, 384)\n\n    cost = np.full((n, n), np.inf) if exact_range else np.zeros((n, n))\n    for i, rn in enumerate(ref_names):\n        for j, sn in enumerate(sub_names):\n            if rn == sn:\n                cost[i, j] = 0.0\n            elif i in matched_ref or j in matched_sub:\n                cost[i, j] = np.inf\n            else:\n                cost_semantic = 1 - util.cos_sim(ref_emb[i], sub_emb[j]).item()\n                if exact_range:\n                    ref_lo, ref_hi = reference_ranges[rn]\n                    sub_lo, sub_hi = submitted_ranges[sn]\n                    if ref_lo != sub_lo or ref_hi != sub_hi:\n                        continue\n                    ref_fracs = sorted(reference_fractions[rn].values())\n                    sub_fracs = sorted(submitted_fractions[sn].values())\n                    if len(ref_fracs) != len(sub_fracs):\n                        continue\n                    cost_fracs = np.sum(np.abs(np.array(ref_fracs) - np.array(sub_fracs)))\n                    \n                    cost[i, j] = cost_fracs*(1-weight_semantic) + cost_semantic*weight_semantic\n                else:\n                    ref_lo, ref_hi = reference_ranges[rn]\n                    sub_lo, sub_hi = submitted_ranges[sn]\n                    scale = max(abs(ref_hi - ref_lo), 1e-6)\n                    cost_range = np.maximum(abs(ref_lo - sub_lo)/scale, abs(ref_hi - sub_hi)/scale)\n                    cost[i, j] = cost_range*(1-weight_semantic) + cost_semantic*weight_semantic\n\n    row_ind, col_ind = linear_sum_assignment(cost)\n    matches = []\n    match_costs = []\n    for i, j in zip(row_ind, col_ind):\n        matches.append((ref_names[i], sub_names[j], float(cost[i, j])))\n        match_costs.append(float(cost[i, j]))\n    return matches, match_costs\n\n\n\n# def old_match_variables_by_hungarian(submitted_ranges, reference_ranges,\n#                                 submitted_fractions=None, reference_fractions=None,\n#                                 exact_range=False):\n#     \"\"\"Find 1-1 matching between variables using the Hungarian algorithm.\n#\n#     If a submitted name exactly matches a reference name, they are forced to match\n#     with cost 0 (and all other pairings involving those names get cost inf).\n#\n#     For unmatched names, cost depends on mode:\n#     - must be within 10% of reference\n    'ntrials_total_ratio': .1, # must be within 10% of reference\n    'T_median_ratio': .1, # must be within 10% of reference\n    'nsubjects_ratio': 0, # must match reference\n    'nneurons_total_ratio': .1, # submitted accuracy must be at least this fraction of reference accuracy for each output dimension\n\ndef test_data_stats(metrics, submitted_data_stats, reference_data_stats):\n\n    fields_check = [\n        'nsessions',\n        'ntrials_total',\n        'T_median',\n        'nsubjects',\n        'nneurons_total',\n    ]\n    \n    for field in fields_check:\n        metrics[field+'_ratio'] = submitted_data_stats[field] / reference_data_stats['data_summary'][field]\n\n    # check that output ranges match exactly, then use fraction error to match\n    output_matches, output_match_costs = match_variables_by_hungarian(\n        submitted_data_stats['output_range'],\n        reference_data_stats['data_summary']['output_range'],\n        submitted_data_stats['output_fractions'],\n        reference_data_stats['data_summary']['output_fractions'],\n        exact_range=True,\n    )\n    metrics['output_matches'] = [\n        {'reference': ref, 'submitted': sub, 'cost': cost}\n        for ref, sub, cost in output_matches\n    ]\n\n    mean_output_fraction_cost = np.mean(output_match_costs)\n    metrics['output_fraction_mean_cost'] = float(mean_output_fraction_cost)\n    sub_output_range = submitted_data_stats['output_range']\n    ref_output_range = reference_data_stats['data_summary']['output_range']\n    for ref_name, sub_name, _ in output_matches:\n        ref_lo, ref_hi = ref_output_range[ref_name]\n        sub_lo, sub_hi = sub_output_range[sub_name]\n        metrics[f'output_range_error_{ref_name}'] = float(max(abs(ref_lo - sub_lo), abs(ref_hi - sub_hi)))\n        # check that the number of inputs and outputs matches the reference\n    assert submitted_data_stats['dinput'] == reference_data_stats['data_summary']['dinput'], f\"dinput in submitted data ({submitted_data_stats['dinput']}) does not match reference ({reference_data_stats['data_summary']['dinput']})\"\n    assert submitted_data_stats['doutput'] == reference_data_stats['data_summary']['doutput'], f\"doutput in submitted data ({submitted_data_stats['doutput']}) does not match reference ({reference_data_stats['data_summary']['doutput']})\"\n\n    # check that we could match inputs with a reasonably low cost, indicating that ranges are similar\n    assert mean_input_cost < STATLIMITS['input_match_cost'], (\n        f\"Input range mean matching cost too high ({mean_input_cost:.3f} >= {STATLIMITS['input_match_cost']}). \"\n        f\"Matches: {[(r, s, f'{c:.3f}') for r, s, c in input_matches]}\"\n    )\n\n    # check that output ranges match\n    inf_matches = [(r, s) for r, s, c in output_matches if np.isinf(c)]\n    assert not inf_matches, (\n        f\"Output ranges do not match exactly for: {inf_matches}. \"\n        f\"All matches: {[(r, s, f'{c:.3f}') for r, s, c in output_matches]}\"\n    )\n\n    # check that output fractions have a reasonably low mean cost, indicating that distributions are similar\n    assert mean_output_fraction_cost < STATLIMITS['output_match_cost'], (\n        f\"Output fractions mean matching cost too high ({mean_output_fraction_cost:.3f} >= {STATLIMITS['output_match_cost']}). \"\n        f\"Matches: {[(r, s, f'{c:.3f}') for r, s, c in output_matches]}\"\n    )\n\ndef test_decoder_accuracy(metrics, submitted_data_full, submitted_data_stats, reference_data_stats):\n    \"\"\"Run the provided train_decoder.py script on the sample data and check that it runs without error.\"\"\"\n\n    train_params = {\n        'npcs': 100,\n        'lr': 1e-3,\n        'l1_weight': 1e-4,\n        'balanced_loss': True,\n    }\n\n    metrics['validation_balanced_accuracy'] = None\n    ref_accuracy = reference_data_stats['validation_balanced_accuracy']\n    metrics['validation_balanced_accuracy_reference'] = ref_accuracy\n    metrics['validation_balanced_accuracy_ratio'] = None\n\n    # train the decoder on the submitted data\n    rng_state = reference_data_stats.get('rng_state', None)\n    try:\n        scores, predictions, pcs, confidences, train_idx, test_idx, model, output, _ = \\\n            train_validate_decoder(submitted_data_full['neural'], submitted_data_full['input'],\n                                submitted_data_full['output'], state=rng_state, **train_params)\n    except torch.cuda.OutOfMemoryError:\n        print(\"CUDA out of memory error during decoder training. Retrying on CPU...\")\n        train_params['device'] = torch.device('cpu')\n        scores, predictions, pcs, confidences, train_idx, test_idx, model, output, _ = \\\n            train_validate_decoder(submitted_data_full['neural'], submitted_data_full['input'],\n                                submitted_data_full['output'], state=rng_state, **train_params)\n\n    # evaluate accuracy on test set    \n    test_predictions_only = get_trial_indices(predictions, test_idx)\n    test_output_only = get_trial_indices(output, test_idx)\n    balanced_accuracy = accuracy_all_sessions(test_predictions_only, test_output_only, balanced=True)\n\n    sub_accuracy = {\n        submitted_data_full['output_names'][i]: float(balanced_accuracy[i])\n        for i in range(len(balanced_accuracy))\n    }\n    metrics['validation_balanced_accuracy'] = sub_accuracy\n    \n    # find a match between output dimensions\n    output_matches, output_match_costs = match_variables_by_hungarian(\n        submitted_data_stats['output_range'],\n        reference_data_stats['data_summary']['output_range'],\n        submitted_data_stats['output_fractions'],\n        reference_data_stats['data_summary']['output_fractions'],\n        exact_range=True,\n    )\n    \n    metrics['output_match_cost'] = float(np.mean(output_match_costs))\n\n    metrics['validation_balanced_accuracy_ratio'] = {\n        ref_name: sub_accuracy[sub_name] / ref_accuracy[ref_name]\n        for ref_name, sub_name, _ in output_matches\n    }\n    \n    for ref_name, sub_name, _ in output_matches:\n        ref_acc = ref_accuracy[ref_name]\n        sub_acc = sub_accuracy[sub_name]\n        assert sub_acc >= MIN_ACCURACY_FRAC * ref_acc, f'Accuracy for output {sub_name} ({sub_acc:.3f}) is below {MIN_ACCURACY_FRAC*100:.1f}% of reference accuracy ({ref_acc:.3f})'\n\n\n    \n","numLines":504,"startLine":1,"totalLines":504}}}

Load existing metrics.json (written by pytest)\n    try:\n        with open(args.metrics_json) as f:\n            metrics = json.load(f)\n    except (FileNotFoundError, json.JSONDecodeError) as e:\n        print(f\"Warning: could not read metrics.json: {e}\", file=sys.stderr)\n        metrics = {}\n\n    # Clear any stale error from a prior run\n    metrics[f\"llm_judge_{args.model_name}_error\"] = \"\"\n\n    # Read LLM judge evaluation\n    try:\n        with open(args.eval_json) as f:\n            eval_data = json.load(f)\n    except (FileNotFoundError, json.JSONDecodeError) as e:\n        print(f\"Error: could not read eval JSON: {e}\", file=sys.stderr)\n        metrics[f\"llm_judge_{args.model_name}_reward\"] = None\n        metrics[f\"llm_judge_{args.model_name}_error\"] = str(e)\n        with open(args.metrics_json, \"w\") as f:\n            json.dump(metrics, f, indent=2)\n        return\n\n    # Score each decision point\n    decision_scores = []\n    code_scores = []\n    details = {}\n\n    for key, entry in eval_data.items():\n        d_cat = entry.get(\"decision_correctness\", \"\").upper()\n        c_cat = entry.get(\"code_correctness\", \"\").upper()\n\n        d_score = DECISION_SCORES.get(d_cat)\n        c_score = CODE_SCORES.get(c_cat)\n\n        if d_score is not None:\n            decision_scores.append(d_score)\n        else:\n            print(f\"Warning: unknown decision_correctness '{d_cat}' for {key}\", file=sys.stderr)\n\n        if c_score is not None:\n            code_scores.append(c_score)\n        else:\n            print(f\"Warning: unknown code_correctness '{c_cat}' for {key}\", file=sys.stderr)\n\n        details[key] = {\n            \"decision_correctness\": d_cat,\n            \"decision_score\": d_score,\n            \"code_correctness\": c_cat,\n            \"code_score\": c_score,\n        }\n\n    mean_decision = sum(decision_scores) / len(decision_scores) if decision_scores else 0.0\n    mean_code = sum(code_scores) / len(code_scores) if code_scores else 0.0\n    llm_judge_reward = 0.7 * mean_decision + 0.3 * mean_code\n\n    # Merge into metrics with model-specific prefix\n    model = args.model_name\n    metrics[f\"llm_judge_{model}_reward\"] = llm_judge_reward\n    metrics[f\"llm_judge_{model}_mean_decision_score\"] = mean_decision\n    metrics[f\"llm_judge_{model}_mean_code_score\"] = mean_code\n    metrics[f\"llm_judge_{model}_n_decision_scores\"] = len(decision_scores)\n    metrics[f\"llm_judge_{model}_n_code_scores\"] = len(code_scores)\n    metrics[f\"llm_judge_{model}_details\"] = details\n\n    with open(args.metrics_json, \"w\") as f:\n        json.dump(metrics, f, indent=2)\n\n    print(f\"LLM Judge Reward: {llm_judge_reward:.3f} (decision: {mean_decision:.3f}, code: {mean_code:.3f})\")\n\n\nif __name__ == \"__main__\":\n    main()\n","numLines":102,"startLine":1,"totalLines":102}}}

session/experiment and uses synchronized timestamp objects rather than raw frame indices directly.\n    47→- For Visual Behavior ophys, neural signals of interest are already available as `dff_traces` and `events`; there is no reference path here that recomputes dF/F from raw fluorescence during analysis-time loading.\n    48→- `BehaviorOphysExperiment.from_lims(..., exclude_invalid_rois=True)` and `cell_specimen_table` docstring explicitly states the returned table contains only valid ROIs after filtering.\n    50→- Trial definitions are encoded in `Trial._get_trial_data`: aborted trials are detected from an `abort` event; if aborted, then `go = catch = auto_rewarded = False`. Catch trials are `trial_params[\"catch\"] is True`; auto-rewarded trials come from `trial_params[\"auto_reward\"]`; go trials are the remaining non-catch, non-auto-rewarded trials.\n    51→- Trial timing uses synchronized stimulus timestamps. The code first subtracts monitor delay when constructing event timestamps for some trial events, then `add_change_time` uses the session stimulus timestamps array to convert `change_frame` to `change_time`.\n    52→- `Trials._get_trial_bounds` adjusts consecutive trial bounds so there is no dead time between trials.\n    53→- `stimulus_presentations` contains more than just the active task block in newer SDK releases; for VBO change-detection analyses, the active block is identified via `stimulus_block_name` containing `change_detection`.\n    54→- `BehaviorSession.running_speed` is sampled on timestamps with monitor delay `0.0`, so running is aligned to sync/stimulus time without display-lag compensation.\n    55→- `BehaviorSession.eye_tracking` / `EyeTrackingTable` use frame timestamps plus blink/outlier filtering; blink frames are set to `NaN` for derived pupil/eye area signals.\n    56→- Relevant session metadata available through SDK include `mouse_id`, |\n   121→| Trials / session | Not stated explicitly in the whitepaper/paper text reviewed | Not explicitly tabulated in text |\n   122→| Neural data time bin | Native imaging rate: 31 Hz single-plane, 11 Hz per plane multi-plane | Whitepaper: “31 Hz for single plane... 11 Hz for each plane in multi-plane experiments” |\n   123→| Behavior data time bin | 30 Hz eye tracking and 30 Hz behavior | Whitepaper: “eye tracking (30 Hz), and behavior (30 Hz)” |\n   124→| Reward rate | Engagement threshold at 2 rewards/min in SDK metrics | Whitepaper/methods: “reward rates above and below 2 rewards per minute” |\n   125→| Omission probability | 5% of non-change stimuli in imaging sessions | Whitepaper: “stimuli were omitted with a 5% probability” |\n   126→| Catch probability | ~12.5% in ~4.2 s after flash alignment | Methods: “mean of 4.25 seconds... resulting in a mean change time of 4.2 seconds” |\n   128→| Response window | 150-750 ms after change/sham change, before display-lag compensation | Methods/whitepaper: “response window (150-750ms before compensating for monitor display lag)” |\n   129→| Stimulus cadence | 250 ms image followed by 500 ms gray screen | Paper: “250 ms stimulus duration... 500 ms inter-stimulus duration” |\n   130→\n   131→\n   132→### Processing Details\n   133→- Task structure in both whitepaper and paper matches the SDK trial logic: continuous flashed-image change detection with `GO`, `CATCH`, aborted early-lick resets, and free-reward/auto-reward trials.\n   134→- Imaging sessions include passive viewing sessions and omission flashes; omissions occur only on non-change stimuli, and neither changes nor the image immediately before a change are omitted.\n   135→- Temporal synchronization was performed by recording experimental clocks on a single NI PCI-6612 digital IO board sampled at 100 kHz.\n   136→- Running speed processing in the whitepaper matches the SDK implementation notes:\n   137→  - unwrap encoder voltage,\n   138→  - remove transients/spikes,\n   139→  - remove z-score >= 10 artifacts,\n   140→  - smooth with a 10 Hz low-pass Butterworth filter for the default running-speed signal.\n   141→- dF/F processing described in the whitepaper:\n   142→  - estimate noise from a median-filtered, truncated centered trace using MAD,\n   143→  - define baseline using a 600 s median filter,\n   144→  - normalize `(raw - baseline)` by baseline, except use noise std when baseline is too small,\n   145→  - detrend with a 3.33 s median filter constrained by noise estimates.\n   146→- The strategy paper states its neural analyses use detected calcium events rather than raw dF/F, and for event-triggered analyses it linearly interpolates onto common 30 Hz timestamps relative to behavioral events.\n   147→- The strategy paper’s decoder analyses:\n   148→  - use random forest classifiers,\n   149→  - decode change-vs-repeat or hit-vs-miss,\n   150→  - operate on the first 400 ms after each stimulus presentation,\n   151→  - use 5-fold cross-validation,\n   152→  - report % correct averaged over imaging planes.\n   153→- This paper’s decoding setup is not identical to the required downstream decoder in this task, but it is still useful as a sanity reference that change and hit information should be decodable above chance from neural activity when alignment is correct.\n   154→\n   155→### Curation Steps\n   156→\n   157→**Neuron curation rules**:\n   158→- Segmentation produces ROIs; non-cell-body ROIs are excluded if they are unions of multiple cells, duplicates, edge/motion-truncated ROIs, likely apical dendrites, or too small/narrow/dim to be confident cells.\n   159→- Duplicate ROIs (>70% overlap) and union ROIs are filtered before demixing; additional problematic ROIs with negative/zero demixed traces and overlapping ROIs are removed, producing a loss of about 1% of ROIs.\n   160→- Pairwise session matching failures with very low matched-cell fraction (<10%) were manually evaluated; sessions not possible to register were excluded from the released dataset.\n   161→\n   162→**Trial curation rules**:\n   163→- Aborted trials are defined by licks before the scheduled change and are excluded from hit/false-alarm/d-prime calculations in the SDK metrics.\n   164→- Free-reward / auto-reward trials occur for the first 5 trials of sessions and after 10 consecutive misses; these should not be treated as standard go/catch contingencies.\n   165→- Omitted stimuli never occur on change stimuli or the image immediately preceding a change.\n   166→\n   167→### Decoders Trained\n   168→| Decoded variable | Accuracy |\n   169→| Image changes vs repeats | Cross-validated RF `% correct`; qualitative result in paper: decodable for all cell classes and both strategies; exact numeric values not stated in body text |\n   170→| Hits vs misses | Cross-validated RF `% correct`; qualitative result in paper: higher for excitatory and Vip cells in visual vs timing strategy sessions |\n   171→| False alarms | Qualitative result in paper: “very low” decoding performance with no strategy difference |\n   172→\n   173→---\n   174→\n   175→## Step 4: Check for Consistency\n   176→**Status**: COMPLETE\n   177→\n   178→### Discrepancies Found\n   179→| Topic | Code says | Data shows | Papers say | Resolution |\n   180→|-------|-----------|------------|------------|------------|\n   181→| Session definition | SDK distinguishes `behavior_session`, `ophys_session`, and `ophys_experiment`; one NWB file corresponds to one `ophys_experiment` | Local data are 284 experiment NWBs but only 247 unique behavior/ophys sessions | Whitepaper says single-plane has 1 experiment/session, multi-plane can have up to 8 experiments/session | Treat each NWB experiment file as one decoder session because neural traces are experiment-specific; preserve subject/session metadata so multiple experiments from one behavior session remain linked through subject/session fields |\n   182→| Dataset size | SDK/project metadata cover the full release tables | Local disk has only 284 NWBs, far fewer than 1,936 experiments in `ophys_experiment_table.csv` | Paper refers to 376 imaging sessions / 82 mice for its study subset; whitepaper describes larger release variants | Interpret local files as a curated subset of the release; later statistics must be compared to the local subset when validating converted data, not to the full public release totals |\n   183→| Neural signal choice | SDK exposes both `dff_traces` file per session/experiment and uses synchronized timestamp objects rather than raw frame indices directly.\n- For Visual Behavior ophys, neural signals of interest are already available as `dff_traces` and `events`; there is no reference path here that recomputes dF/F from raw fluorescence during analysis-time loading.\n- `BehaviorOphysExperiment.from_lims(..., are detected from an `abort` event; if aborted, then `go = catch = auto_rewarded = False`. Catch trials are `trial_params[\"catch\"] is True`; auto-rewarded trials come from `trial_params[\"auto_reward\"]`; go trials are the remaining non-catch, non-auto-rewarded trials.\n- Trial timing uses synchronized stimulus timestamps. The code first subtracts monitor delay when constructing event timestamps for some trial events, then `add_change_time` uses the session stimulus timestamps array to convert `change_frame` to `change_time`.\n- `Trials._get_trial_bounds` adjusts consecutive trial bounds so there is no dead time between trials.\n- `stimulus_presentations` contains more than just the active task block in newer SDK releases; for VBO change-detection analyses, the active block is identified via `stimulus_block_name` containing `change_detection`.\n- `BehaviorSession.running_speed` is sampled on timestamps with monitor delay `0.0`, so running is aligned to sync/stimulus time without display-lag compensation.\n- `BehaviorSession.eye_tracking` / `EyeTrackingTable` use frame timestamps plus blink/outlier filtering; blink frames are set to `NaN` for derived pupil/eye area signals.\n- Relevant session metadata available through SDK include `mouse_id`, `targeted_structure`, `ophys_frame_rate`, `behavior_session_id`, and `ophys_session_id`, which are likely useful for subject and brain-region mapping in the conversion.\n\n---\n\n## Step 2: Dataset Exploration\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\n### Data Structure\n- Top-level data directory contains:\n  - `visual-behavior-ophys_project_manifest_v1.1.0.json`\n  - `_manifest_last_used.txt`\n  - `_downloaded_data.json`\n  - `visual-behavior-ophys-1.1.0/behavior_ophys_experiments/*.nwb`\n  - `visual-behavior-ophys-1.1.0/project_metadata/*.csv`\n- Local data are a subset of the Visual Behavior Ophys 1.1.0 release.\n- Primary session files are NWB/HDF5 files, one per `ophys_experiment_id`.\n- Metadata CSVs:\n  - `behavior_session_table.csv`: per behavior session metadata and behavioral trial counts\n  - `ophys_session_table.csv`: per ophys session metadata; actual mean ~4.2 s after flash alignment | Methods: “mean of 4.25 seconds... resulting in a mean change time of 4.2 seconds” |\n| Response window | 150-750 ms after change/sham change, before display-lag compensation | Methods/whitepaper: “response window (150-750ms before compensating for monitor display lag)” |\n| Stimulus cadence | 250 ms image followed by 500 ms gray screen | Paper: “250 ms stimulus duration... 500 ms inter-stimulus duration” |\n\n\n### Processing Details\n- Task structure in both whitepaper and paper matches the SDK trial logic: continuous flashed-image change detection with `GO`, `CATCH`, aborted early-lick resets, and free-reward/auto-reward trials.\n- Imaging sessions include passive viewing sessions and omission flashes; omissions occur only on non-change stimuli, and neither changes nor the image immediately before a change are omitted.\n- Temporal synchronization was performed by recording experimental clocks on a single NI PCI-6612 digital IO board sampled at 100 kHz.\n- Running speed processing in the whitepaper matches the SDK implementation notes:\n  - unwrap encoder voltage,\n  - remove transients/spikes,\n  - remove z-score >= 10 artifacts,\n  - smooth with a 10 Hz low-pass Butterworth filter for the default running-speed signal.\n- dF/F processing described in the whitepaper:\n  - estimate noise from a median-filtered, truncated centered trace using MAD,\n  - define baseline using a 600 s median filter,\n  - normalize `(raw - baseline)` by baseline, except use noise std when baseline is too small,\n  - detrend with a 3.33 s median filter constrained by noise estimates.\n- The strategy paper states its neural analyses use detected calcium events rather than raw dF/F, and for event-triggered analyses it linearly interpolates onto common 30 Hz timestamps relative to behavioral events.\n- The strategy paper’s decoder analyses:\n  - use random forest classifiers,\n  - decode change-vs-repeat or hit-vs-miss,\n  - operate on the first 400 ms after each stimulus presentation,\n  - use 5-fold cross-validation,\n  - report % correct averaged over imaging planes.\n- This paper’s decoding setup is not identical to the required downstream decoder in this task, but it is still useful as a sanity reference that change and hit information should be decodable above chance from neural activity when alignment is correct.\n\n### Curation Steps\n\n**Neuron curation rules**:\n- Segmentation produces ROIs; non-cell-body ROIs are excluded if they are unions of multiple cells, duplicates, edge/motion-truncated ROIs, likely apical dendrites, or too small/narrow/dim to be confident cells.\n- Duplicate ROIs (>70% overlap) and union ROIs are filtered before demixing; additional problematic ROIs with negative/zero demixed traces and overlapping ROIs are removed, producing a loss of about 1% of ROIs.\n- Pairwise session matching failures with very low matched-cell fraction (<10%) were manually evaluated; sessions not possible to register were excluded from the released dataset.\n\n**Trial curation rules**:\n- Aborted trials are defined by licks before the scheduled change and are excluded from hit/false-alarm/d-prime calculations in the SDK metrics.\n- Free-reward / auto-reward trials occur for the first 5 trials of sessions and after 10 consecutive misses; these should not be treated as standard go/catch contingencies.\n- Omitted stimuli never occur on change stimuli or the image immediately preceding a change.\n\n### Decoders Trained\n| Decoded variable | Accuracy |\n| Image changes vs repeats | Cross-validated RF `% correct`; qualitative result in paper: decodable for all cell classes and both strategies; exact numeric values not stated in body text |\n| Hits vs misses | Cross-validated RF `% correct`; qualitative result in paper: higher for excitatory and Vip cells in visual vs timing strategy sessions |\n| False alarms | Qualitative result in paper: “very low” decoding performance with no strategy difference |\n\n---\n\n## Step 4: Check for Consistency\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\n### Discrepancies Found\n| Topic | Code says | Data shows | Papers say | Resolution |\n|-------|-----------|------------|------------|------------|\n| Session definition | SDK distinguishes `behavior_session`, `ophys_session`, and `ophys_experiment`; Passive sessions also produce degenerate outcomes (sample passive file: all go trials are misses and all catch trials are correct rejects). Excluding passive sessions keeps the mapping aligned to the operant task.\n   209→2. **Use `events` instead of `dff_traces`**: The whitepaper explains dF/F generation, but the strategy paper explicitly states its neural analyses used detected calcium events. Events are already present in the NWB files and best match the reference analyses.\n   210→3. **Use a common 30 Hz time base for all sessions**: The target format requires one shared bin size across sessions, while local data mix ~31 Hz single-plane and ~11 Hz multi-plane ophys sampling. The strategy paper linearly interpolates calcium event responses onto common 30 Hz timestamps for neural and running analyses, and behavior/eye tracking are naturally 30 Hz, so 30 Hz is the most defensible common grid.\n   211→4. **Segment trials from `start_time` to `stop_time`**: Trial boundaries will come directly from the NWB `intervals/trials` table, after filtering to keep only `(go or catch) and not aborted and not auto_rewarded`.\n   212→5. **Represent all outputs as time-varying**: To satisfy the decoder format and simplify training, even static trial outcome will be repeated across all bins in a trial.\n   213→6. **Use `gray` as an explicit image-identity class**: Because the task includes 500 ms gray periods and omissions extend gray instead of showing an image, a `gray` category is needed for a complete time-varying identity signal.\n   214→7. **Discretize continuous outputs globally, not per session**: Running-speed and pupil-diameter bin edges will be computed from all valid included timepoints across the converted dataset so class definitions are shared across sessions.\n   215→8. **Require at least two valid trials per session after filtering**: The active-session subset already satisfies this in a spot/global check (202 active sessions; minimum 39 valid trials after filtering), so no expected extra session loss from this rule.\n   216→9. **Load NWB content with `h5py` rather than the SDK session object**: The reference SDK is still the guide for field semantics and processing, but the current environment’s `pynwb/hdmf` stack cannot instantiate these NWB 2.6.0 files through `BehaviorOphysExperiment.from_nwb_path` because of an `external_resources` abstract-method mismatch. Direct HDF5 reads will therefore mirror the SDK field definitions explicitly.\n   217→\n   218→### Planned Sanity Checks\n   219→- [ ] For a chosen session/trial/neuron, compare the converted neural They are therefore not fixable without changing the referenced neural representation.\n   392→2. **Constructed sanity checks using raw data and `np.allclose()`**:\n   393→   - Neural sanity check 1:\n   394→     - Session `775614751`, trial 0: converted neural matrix vs direct interpolation of raw NWB event traces onto the raw trial grid.\n   395→     - Result: `np.allclose == True` with `atol=1e-6`.\n   396→   - Neural sanity check 2:\n   397→     - Session `939327156`, trial 10: same comparison.\n   398→     - Result: `np.allclose == True`.\n   399→   - Input sanity check:"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"53d11b8f-13f2-4ed5-8e42-042a9c28b3ea","uuid":"dc38a2df-70d1-4a65-bc37-883fe3a6dfc1","tool_use_result":{"type":"text","file":{"filePath":"/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md","content":"| stimulus-presentation `is_change`, `start_time`, `stop_time` | `output[image_change]` | Binary time-varying label: 1 during the changed-image presentation window, else 0 | `get_stimulus_presentations`; trial `change_time` logic in `Trial._get_trial_timing` | Marks the post-change flashed image itself rather than a one-bin impulse; catch trials remain 0 |\n| running speed timeseries | `output[running_speed_bin]` | Interpolate running speed onto 30 Hz trial grid; discretize with global quintile bins across included active-session timepoints | `BehaviorSession.running_speed`, `RunningSpeed.from_stimulus_file` | Uses filtered running speed, matching SDK default |\n| eye-tracking pupil width/height + blink mask | `output[pupil_diameter_bin]` | Compute pupil diameter as `max(width, height)`; use blink-masked values; interpolate across valid timestamps onto 30 Hz trial grid; discretize with global quintile bins | `EyeTrackingTable`, `filter_on_blinks` | Small blink-related gaps are filled by interpolation after applying reference invalid-frame masking |\n| trial outcome flags (`hit`, `miss`, `false_alarm`, `correct_reject`) | `output[trial_outcome]` | Static trial label, broadcast across trial timepoints on 30 Hz grid | `Trial._get_trial_data` | Categories: `hit`, `miss`, `false_alarm`, `correct_reject` |\n| NWB `general/subject/subject_id` / metadata `mouse_id` | `subjects`, `subject_idx` | Convert to global subject list and per-session index | `BehaviorSession.metadata` | Use mouse identifier strings |\n| experiment metadata `targeted_structure` | `brain_regions`, `brain_region_idx` | Single region per file repeated for all neurons in that session | `BehaviorOphysExperiment.metadata` | Included regions expected locally: `VISp`, `VISl` |\n\n### Key Decisions\n1. **Use only active sessions**: Local data include 82 passive experiment files, but the decoder task requires trial outcome and the strategy paper’s behavioral analyses focus on active sessions. Passive sessions also produce degenerate outcomes (sample passive file: all go trials are misses and all catch trials are correct rejects). Excluding passive sessions keeps the mapping aligned to the operant task.\n2. **Use `events` instead of `dff_traces`**: The whitepaper explains dF/F generation, but the strategy paper explicitly states its neural analyses used detected calcium events. Events are already present in the NWB files and best match the reference analyses.\n3. **Use a common 30 Hz time base for all sessions**: The target format requires one shared bin size across sessions, while local data mix ~31 Hz single-plane and ~11 Hz multi-plane ophys sampling. The strategy paper linearly interpolates calcium event responses onto common 30 Hz timestamps for neural and running analyses, and behavior/eye tracking are naturally 30 Hz, so 30 Hz is the most defensible common grid.\n4. **Segment trials from `start_time` to `stop_time`**: Trial boundaries will come directly from the NWB `intervals/trials` table, after filtering to keep only `(go or catch) and not aborted and not auto_rewarded`.\n5. **Represent all outputs as time-varying**: To satisfy the decoder format and simplify training, even static trial outcome will be repeated across all bins in a trial.\n6. **Use `gray` as an explicit image-identity class**: Because the task includes 500 ms gray periods and omissions extend gray instead of showing an image, a `gray` category is needed for a complete time-varying identity signal.\n7. **Discretize continuous outputs globally, not per session**: Running-speed and pupil-diameter bin edges will be computed from all valid included timepoints across the converted dataset so class definitions are shared across sessions.\n8. **Require at least two valid trials per session after filtering**: The active-session subset already satisfies this in a spot/global check (202 active sessions; minimum 39 valid trials after filtering), so no expected extra session loss from this rule.\n9. **Load NWB content with `h5py` rather than the SDK session object**: The reference SDK is still the guide for field semantics and processing, but the current environment’s `pynwb/hdmf` stack cannot instantiate these NWB 2.6.0 files through `BehaviorOphysExperiment.from_nwb_path` because of an `external_resources` abstract-method mismatch. Direct HDF5 reads will therefore mirror the SDK field definitions explicitly.\n\n### Planned Sanity Checks\n- [ ] For a chosen session/trial/neuron, compare the converted neural trial trace against direct interpolation of the raw NWB event trace at the same timestamps with `np.allclose()`.\n- [ ] For a chosen session/trial, compare converted image-identity labels against the raw stimulus-presentation intervals (`image_name`, `omitted`, gray periods) with `np.allclose()` on integer-coded labels.\n- [ ] For a chosen session/trial/timepoint, compare converted running-speed-bin labels to bins obtained from raw running-speed interpolation and the saved global quintile edges.\n- [ ] For a chosen session/trial/timepoint, compare converted pupil-diameter-bin labels to bins obtained from raw blink-masked pupil width/height interpolation and the saved global quintile edges.\n- [ ] Verify per-session valid-trial counts after conversion equal counts computed directly from raw trial flags.\n- [ ] Verify neuron counts per converted session equal the raw event/cell-table ROI counts in the NWB file.\n\n---\n\n## Step 6: Script Development\n**Status**: How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. The AI includes only \"go\" and \"catch\" trials and excludes \"aborted\" and \"auto_rewarded\" trials. Additionally, sessions with fewer than 2 valid trials are excluded.\n\nii.\n```python\nkeep = (trials[\"go\"] | trials[\"catch\"]) & (~trials[\"aborted\"]) & (~trials[\"auto_rewarded\"])\n...\nif int(raw[\"keep_mask\"].sum()) < 2:\n    print(f\"[pass2] skipping session {session.ophys_experiment_id} ...\")\n    continue\n```\n\niii. The AI justified this filtering rule based on the instructions (\"Include both the 'Go' and 'Catch' trials, but exclude the 'Aborted' and 'Auto-rewarded' trials\") and the SDK's trial logic which explicitly defines these categories.\n\n## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the `neural` data derived from?\n\ni. The neural data is derived from the precomputed calcium event detection traces stored at `processing/ophys/event_detection/data` in the NWB files, along with associated timestamps at `processing/ophys/event_detection/timestamps`.\n\nii.\n```python\nophys_timestamps = np.asarray(\n    h5f[\"processing\"][\"ophys\"][\"event_detection\"][\"timestamps\"], dtype=np.float64\n)\nevents = np.asarray(\n    h5f[\"processing\"][\"ophys\"][\"event_detection\"][\"data\"], dtype=np.float32\n)\n```\n\niii. The AI justified using `events` (detected calcium events) rather than `dff_traces` because the strategy paper explicitly states its neural analyses used detected calcium events, making this the best match to the reference processing.\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. The raw event traces (shape: n_timepoints x n_neurons on ophys timestamps) are linearly interpolated onto a common 30 Hz time grid defined by the trial's `start_time` and `stop_time`. The interpolated matrix is then transposed to (n_neurons, n_timepoints).\n\nii.\n```python\ndef interpolate_matrix(source_t, source_values, query_t):\n    right = np.searchsorted(source_t, query_t, side=\"left\")\n    right = np.clip(right, 0, n_src - 1)\n    left = np.clip(right - 1, 0, n_src - 1)\n    ...\n    out = left_vals * (1.0 - w[:, None]) + right_vals * w[:, None]\n    return out.astype(np.float32)\n...\nneural_trial = interpolate_matrix(\n    raw[\"ophys_timestamps\"], raw[\"events\"], grid\n).T.astype(np.float32)\n```\n\niii. The AI justified the 30 Hz interpolation based on the strategy paper which \"linearly interpolates onto common 30 Hz timestamps relative to behavioral events.\"\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. The AI filters neurons using the `valid_roi` flag from the NWB's `cell_specimen_table`. If the number of valid ROIs differs from the total number of event trace columns, only valid ROI columns are kept.\n\nii.\n```python\ncell_table = h5f[\"processing\"][\"ophys\"][\"image_segmentation\"][\"cell_specimen_table\"]\nn_cell_table = len(cell_table[\"id\"])\nif events.shape[1] == n_cell_table and \"valid_roi\" in cell_table:\n    valid_roi = np.asarray(h5_array(cell_table[\"valid_roi\"])).astype(bool)\n    if valid_roi.sum() != events.shape[1]:\n        events = events[:, valid_roi]\nn_neurons = events.shape[1]\n```\n\niii. The AI justified this by referencing the SDK's `CellSpecimens.__init__(..., exclude_invalid_rois=True)` behavior which filters the ROI table to `valid_roi == True`. The CONVERSION_NOTES state: \"Keep only valid ROIs / cells from the released NWB content; do not add extra ad hoc neuron filtering beyond reference QC.\"\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. The neural data is aligned to the trial's `start_time`. Each trial's time grid starts at `start_time` and extends to `stop_time` in steps of 1/30 s. The metadata records `temporal_alignment_event: \"trial start\"` and `off_start: 0.0`.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef session_grid(start, stop, dt=TIME_BIN_SIZE_S):\n    n_bins = max(1, int(math.ceil((stop - start) / dt)))\n    return start + np.arange(n_bins, dtype=np.float64) * dt\n...\nstart = float(raw[\"trials\"][\"start_time\"][trial_idx])\nstop = float(raw[\"trials\"][\"stop_time\"][trial_idx])\ngrid = session_grid(start, stop)\n```\n\niii. The instructions state to \"Temporally align based on ophys timestamp\" which is ambiguous. The AI chose to align to trial start using ophys-synchronized timestamps as the time base, which is a reasonable interpretation since all data streams are already synchronized via the ophys timestamp framework.\n\n## 2-e. What is the temporal resolution (time bin size) of the converted data? Is any temporal rebinning applied?\n\ni. The temporal resolution is 1/30 s (approximately 33.33 ms). All data streams are interpolated onto this common 30 Hz grid. This constitutes rebinning from the native ophys rate (~31 Hz single-plane or ~11 Hz multi-plane) and from the native behavior/eye-tracking rates.\n\nii.\n```python\nTIME_BIN_SIZE_S = 1.0 / 30.0\nTIME_BIN_SIZE_MS = TIME_BIN_SIZE_S * 1000.0\n```\n\niii. The AI justified the 30 Hz time base because the strategy paper \"linearly interpolates calcium event responses onto common 30 Hz timestamps\" and behavior/eye tracking are natively 30 Hz.\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Image identity* derived from?\n\ni. Image identity is derived from the stimulus presentations table in the NWB file, specifically the `image_name`, `start_time`, `stop_time`, and `omitted` columns from the active task presentation group.\n\nii.\n```python\nstim = read_interval_table(stim_group, [\n    \"start_time\", \"stop_time\", \"image_name\", \"is_change\", \"omitted\",\n    \"trials_id\", \"active\", \"flashes_since_change\",\n])\n```\n\niii. The AI documented this in the variable mapping as using \"stimulus-presentation `image_name`, `start_time`, `stop_time`, `omitted`, active task block only.\"\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Image identity*?\n\ni. A categorical time-varying signal is constructed on the 30 Hz grid. For each time point, the code finds which stimulus presentation interval contains it (using `searchsorted`). If the time point falls within a non-omitted image presentation (between `start_time` and `stop_time`), the corresponding `image_name` is encoded as an integer. Otherwise, the time point is labeled as \"gray\" (code 0). The global category list is `[\"gray\"] + sorted(unique_image_names)`.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef stimulus_identity_codes(stimulus, query_t, image_to_code):\n    idx = np.searchsorted(starts, query_t, side=\"right\") - 1\n    codes = np.full(query_t.shape, image_to_code[\"gray\"], dtype=np.int64)\n    valid = idx >= 0\n    valid &= idx < len(starts)\n    idx_valid = idx[valid]\n    in_interval = query_t[valid] < stops[idx_valid]\n    if np.any(in_interval):\n        sub_idx = idx_valid[in_interval]\n        names = np.asarray(image_names[sub_idx], dtype=object)\n        omit = omitted[sub_idx]\n        target = np.full(sub_idx.shape, image_to_code[\"gray\"], dtype=np.int64)\n        for i, (name, is_omitted) in enumerate(zip(names, omit)):\n            if (not is_omitted) and str(name) in image_to_code:\n                target[i] = image_to_code[str(name)]\n        assign = np.flatnonzero(valid)[in_interval]\n        codes[assign] = target\n    return codes\n```\n\niii. The AI justified the explicit \"gray\" category because \"the task includes 500 ms gray periods and omissions extend gray instead of showing an image, a gray category is needed for a complete time-varying identity signal.\"\n\n## 3-c. How is `output` *Image identity* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Image identity is computed on the same 30 Hz time grid as the neural data, using the same `grid` array derived from trial `start_time` and `stop_time`. Both neural and stimulus data are sampled at the same absolute times.\n\nii.\n```python\ngrid = session_grid(start, stop)\nneural_trial = interpolate_matrix(raw[\"ophys_timestamps\"], raw[\"events\"], grid).T\n...\nimage_codes = stimulus_identity_codes(raw[\"stimulus\"], grid, image_to_code)\n```\n\niii. Alignment is ensured by computing all outputs on the same `grid` as the neural data.\n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Image change* derived from?\n\ni. Image change is derived from the `is_change` and `omitted` columns of the stimulus presentations table, along with `start_time` and `stop_time`.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef stimulus_change_codes(stimulus, query_t):\n    starts = stimulus[\"start_time\"]\n    stops = stimulus[\"stop_time\"]\n    is_change = stimulus[\"is_change\"]\n    omitted = stimulus[\"omitted\"]\n```\n\niii. The AI documented this as using \"stimulus-presentation `is_change`, `start_time`, `stop_time`.\"\n\n## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Image change*?\n\ni. A binary time-varying signal is constructed on the 30 Hz grid. For each time point, the code checks whether it falls within a stimulus presentation interval that is marked as `is_change=True` (and not omitted). If so, the value is 1; otherwise 0. This means the \"change\" label spans the entire duration of the changed-image presentation window (~250 ms = ~7-8 bins).\n\nii.\n```python\ndef stimulus_change_codes(stimulus, query_t):\n    idx = np.searchsorted(starts, query_t, side=\"right\") - 1\n    codes = np.zeros(query_t.shape, dtype=np.int64)\n    ...\n    sub_idx = idx_valid[in_interval]\n    changed = is_change[sub_idx] & (~omitted[sub_idx])\n    assign = np.flatnonzero(valid)[in_interval]\n    codes[assign] = changed.astype(np.int64)\n    return codes\n```\n\niii. The AI initially used a single-bin impulse at `change_time` but found this was \"too sparse\" and caused below-chance decoder accuracy. They switched to marking the entire changed-image presentation window, documented as a design change in CONVERSION_NOTES Step 10.\n\n## 4-c. How is `output` *Image change* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Image change is already a binary variable (0 = no change, 1 = change) and does not require additional thresholding.\n\nii.\n```python\n[\"no_change\", \"change\"],\n```\n\niii. The instructions specify \"Image change, binary variable. Have value of 1 right after a change in image identity, otherwise 0.\"\n\n## 4-d. How is `output` *Image change* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Image change is computed on the same 30 Hz `grid` as the neural data, using the same trial time grid.\n\nii.\n```python\nimage_change = stimulus_change_codes(raw[\"stimulus\"], grid)\n```\n\niii. Same alignment approach as all other outputs.\n\n## 5-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Running speed* derived from?\n\ni. Running speed is derived from `processing/running/speed/data` and `processing/running/speed/timestamps` in the NWB file.\n\nii.\n```python\nrunning_speed = np.asarray(h5f[\"processing\"][\"running\"][\"speed\"][\"data\"], dtype=np.float32)\nrunning_timestamps = np.asarray(\n    h5f[\"processing\"][\"running\"][\"speed\"][\"timestamps\"], dtype=np.float64\n)\n```\n\niii. The AI documented that this uses the SDK-equivalent filtered running speed signal.\n\n## 5-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Running speed*?\n\ni. The raw running speed is linearly interpolated onto the trial's 30 Hz time grid using `np.interp`. Then it is discretized into 5 equal-percentile bins using global quintile edges computed across all valid trial timepoints in the dataset.\n\nii.\n```python\nrunning_cont = interpolate_vector(raw[\"running_timestamps\"], raw[\"running_speed\"], grid)\n...\nrunning_bins = digitize_with_edges(running_cont, running_edges)\n```\n\niii. The AI justified using global quintile edges to ensure \"class definitions are shared across sessions.\"\n\n## 5-c. How is `output` *Running speed* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Running speed is discretized into 5 bins (0-4) using the 20th, 40th, 60th, and 80th percentile values computed globally across all valid timepoints. `np.digitize` maps continuous values to bin indices.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef robust_quintile_edges(values):\n    percentiles = np.nanpercentile(values, [20, 40, 60, 80]).astype(np.float64)\n    for i in range(1, len(percentiles)):\n        if percentiles[i] <= percentiles[i - 1]:\n            percentiles[i] = percentiles[i - 1] + 1e-6\n    return percentiles\n\ndef digitize_with_edges(values, edges):\n    return np.digitize(values, edges, right=False).astype(np.int64)\n```\n\niii. The instructions specify \"discretized into five equal percentile bins.\" The AI adds a small epsilon to prevent degenerate edges where percentiles coincide.\n\n## 5-d. How is `output` *Running speed* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Pupil diameter*?\n\ni. Pupil diameter is computed as `max(width, height)` of the pupil tracking ellipse fit. NaN values (from blinks or invalid frames) are handled by interpolating only over finite samples. The valid pupil diameter values are linearly interpolated onto the trial's 30 Hz time grid, then discretized into 5 equal-percentile bins using global quintile edges.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef interpolate_pupil(pupil_t, pupil_diameter, query_t):\n    valid = np.isfinite(pupil_diameter)\n    if valid.sum() == 0:\n        raise ValueError(\"No valid pupil samples available\")\n    if valid.sum() == 1:\n        return np.full(query_t.shape, float(pupil_diameter[valid][0]), dtype=np.float32)\n    return np.interp(query_t, pupil_t[valid], pupil_diameter[valid]).astype(np.float32)\n```\n\niii.
